# Tahdhīb al-Manṭiq — cumulative H later-gloss corpus, THREE independent historical numberings

**Stage111 · 9 November 2026 · GNAMMA research working edition · NOT independently manuscript-collated or verified against page photographs.**

**Three sections with distinct historical local note numbers and permanent editorial IDs:**

- **H-PREFACE:** Qum pp127–164, notes **HWN-001–140**: 136 complete first-pass English, four partial (1,25,82,90), 54 digitally searchable full-Arabic working note transcriptions and 86 locator-only; six unnumbered source quotations.
- **H-INTRO:** Qum pp165–187, notes **HWN-INTRO-001–082**: 66 first-pass complete English, 16 partial; **zero full Arabic transcripts** (82 locator-only); three separate unnumbered source notes.
- **H-DALALA:** Qum pp188–191, notes **HWN-DALALA-001–020**: 14 complete first-pass English, six partial; **zero full Arabic transcripts** (20 locator-only). Qum p188 explicitly restarts local numbering with the rubric `حواشي التصورات « بحث الدلالات »`.

**Combined:** **242 DISTINCT historical numbered records; 216 first-pass complete English and 26 partial.** Nine separately attributed unnumbered passages. Only **54/242** notes contain full *digitally searchable* Arabic, which remains unphotographed; **188/242** contain only short Arabic locator phrases. **0/274 full later-H printed pages** photographically collated/definitively translated; **0/110 main al-Yazdī positions** independently manuscript-certified. Notes and pages are not interchangeable. A first-pass English summary is not a critical translation of every source Arabic clause. The bound H material continues beyond page191 to page400.

**Do not treat these separate note sequences as one historical count extending 1–242.** Separate editorial ID namespaces are necessary because the printed book restarts at p165 and again at p188. No manuscripts or reported hadith are independently verified in this file.

## PREFACE — local historical numbering

# Tahdhīb al-Manṭiq — cumulative Arabic–English later preface glosses, notes 001–140

**GNAMMA Stage109 · 9 November 2026 · Ḥawāshī al-ḥāshiya · preliminary scholarly English working corpus.**

## Important section boundary: numbering resets after these 140 notes

**This is the COMPLETE numbered-note sequence of the LATER PREFACE GLOSSES, Qum printed pp.127–164.** Qum printed **p.165 begins a NEW section titled `حواشي مقدمة علم المنطق` (Glosses on the Introduction to Logic) and starts its OWN local note (1)**. Do not invent global note (141) for p165. The next section must have separate stable IDs, e.g. `HWN-INTRO-001` and a distinct source/section register. The fact that these 140 prefatory notes have now been indexed does **not** mean the 274 printed positions of later H glosses are fully translated.

## Evidence-graded coverage

- **140 numbered later-preface glosses** currently indexed, with **136 complete FIRST-PASS English of their digitally visible numbered units** and four incomplete (**1,25,82,90**).
- **54 full working digital searchable-Arabic note transcriptions**, specifically 24–75 and 114–115; the other **86 notes contain SHORT ARABIC LOCATOR LEMMATA ONLY** and are not a complete Arabic–English transcription.
- **SIX UNNUMBERED adjacent glosses** are saved separately after notes 47,48,71,86,90,112, not counted as numbered notes.
- The latest numbered-note English fields total approximately **22,561 words**, including partial note renderings. Printed-page photos, independent manuscripts, variant stems and quoted hadith authentications have NOT been verified by this working text.
- **0/274 H later printed positions fully translated AND independently source-collated**. The principal al-Yazdī Y commentary is a *different textual family* Q7–123, 110/110 substantive positions working-translated but **0/110** independently manuscript-certified. The original Taftāzānī T matn is a third family.

**Source links are to digitally searchable Qum printed pages; they do not certify photographs.** Book-level catalogue attribution to al-Yazdī must not be projected onto these later, sometimes very late voices.

## Note 1 — PARTIAL / unverified text

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [127](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/127). **Reported voice:** Anonymous opening gloss; additional later editorial citations. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قيل الوجه في كتابة البسملة بحذف الألف

</div>

### English — PARTIAL, not complete

One explanation given for writing the basmala without the alif expected by ordinary orthography is the frequency with which the formula is used; the elongated bāʾ is said to compensate graphically for the omission. A report recounts the gradual development of the introductory formula: an earlier expression attributed to Quraysh; then the formula invoking God's name; then adding al-Raḥmān; and finally the full formula with al-Raḥīm. The note offers alternative syntactic expansions: “I begin in the name of God,” “my beginning is in the name of God,” or an understood imperative “Begin” or “Say.” These alternatives change whether the omitted governing expression is verbal, nominal, or understood as an implied predicate. It also reports rival etymologies of ism, either from elevation (sumuww) or from a mark/sign (sima). The bracketed passages in the Qum digital text explicitly reference later authors, including Muṭahharī and al-Ṣāfī, and require separate text-historical treatment rather than attribution to an early glossator.

### Editorial/source qualification

SELECTED substantive rendering, not an exhaustive translation of the modern bracketed interpolations and quoted reports. Hadith-history claims not verified.

<a id="hwn-002"></a>
## Note 2 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [127](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/127) · [128](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/128). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī (signature at the end of Q128 continuation). **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قول المصنف الحمد لله

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On the author's words “Praise belongs to God”: he changed from a verbal sentence to a nominal one to signify permanence and continuance, as befits discourse about the knowing Sovereign. Praise has been placed first because of the special attention it receives in the context, even though God is more important in Himself. Priority determined by a particular circumstance outweighs essential priority in constructing this sentence. The Qurʾānic example in which “to God” is placed before “praise” differs: there the principal intention is to affirm the exclusivity of praise to God, not merely to establish that He is praised.

An objection arises: al-Zamakhsharī has stated that something is called “advanced” or “delayed” only when its position has been changed, not when it has remained in its original place. How, then, can one call the opening word ḥamd advanced?

I reply: the author answers this in his commentary on the Talkhīṣ by distinguishing two kinds of preposing. First, there is preposing with the intention that the word has an underlying later position, as when the predicate precedes its subject. Second, there is preposing without any such intended later position, as when an expression is put first as the subject of a nominal sentence. Thus one may take the noun Zayd, place it before the verb and make it a subject—“Zayd rose”—or put it after the verb and make it the agent—“Rose Zayd.” The advancement of ḥamd is of the second kind, whereas the remark by the author of al-Kashshāf concerns the first. There is no contradiction. Al-Zamakhsharī's own discourse repeatedly calls the first kind “preposing.”

One can also propose a different derivation. The original sentence was “I praise God with praise” (aḥmadu llāha ḥamdan). For the purpose already mentioned, the verbal noun was changed from accusative to nominative, and then ḥamd was placed before the prepositional complement. On that analysis it falls under the first kind of preposing, and no difficulty arises in any event. Reflect.

### Editorial/source qualification

Stage105 supersedes Stage104's abridged note 2. First-pass full numbered-note English from Q127–128; no photo-collation.

<a id="hwn-003"></a>
## Note 3 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [128](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/128). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

الظرف اما لغو متعلق بافتتح

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The prepositional phrase may be an ordinary adjunct governed by “he opened”: he opened his book with praise of God after first seeking blessing through the basmala. Alternatively, it may be a predicative adjunct depending on an omitted “while accompanied by,” with the complement of “opened” also suppressed: he opened his book with the basmala while accompanying it thereafter with praise of God. On this second account, repeating “the basmala” places an explicit noun where a pronoun could have stood. Under the first construal, “the best of speech” means the best speech *other than* the basmala; under the second, the best without qualification. The two constructions also allow different introductory reports to be invoked: one enjoins praise and the other the naming of God. Under the first, saying that one opened with praise even though praise follows the basmala either focuses on the less obvious fact and omits the obvious, or signals that praise can properly be called an opening despite coming second. Thus the introductory traditions need not contradict one another. The commentator has given a general indication of the distinction that he will subsequently unpack when he asks how the reports concerning the opening can be reconciled. Explication after an initial allusion is more agreeable and effective for the hearer.

### Editorial/source qualification

Complete first-pass rendering of the digitally visible numbered note 3, not image-certified.

<a id="hwn-004"></a>
## Note 4 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [128](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/128). **Reported voice:** Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

البسملة اما ان يقرء بكسر الباء والميم

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The word basmala could, it has been said, be pronounced with kasra on both bāʾ and mīm, treating it as an abbreviated form of “in God's name”; or with fatḥa on both, treating it as an artificially coined verbal noun derived from the formula, just as ḥawqala is derived from “There is no power or strength except through God,” and haylala from “There is no god but God.” But the form with a final tāʾ, which is current in speech and writing, conflicts with the first explanation: on that derivation it would have to be pronounced with a final hāʾ rather than tāʾ.

### Editorial/source qualification

Source gives the rival pronunciations as reported proposals; no claim that either is the author's preferred vocalisation.

<a id="hwn-005"></a>
## Note 5 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [128](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/128). **Reported voice:** Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

منصوب على أنه مفعول له لقوله افتتح

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The expression is accusative because it serves as the object expressing purpose for the verb “he opened.” The adjective khayr (“better/best”) was originally akhyar, just as sharr (“worse/worst”) was originally asharr, but the two normally appear without their initial hamza. Rare evidence preserves the fuller forms: the verse “Bilāl is the best of men and the son of the best” and a reading of the Qurʾānic description of a grievous liar with the original initial hamza.

### Editorial/source qualification

The referent of the unstated accusative is recoverable from the parent lemma but should be fixed in photographed layout.

<a id="hwn-007"></a>
## Note 7 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [128](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/128). **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb, p.6, quoting reports and Ibn al-Athīr. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

كل امر ذي بال لا يبدئ فيه بحمد الله

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On the phrase “through a report from the best of mankind”: it is related from the Prophet that any matter of consequence not begun with God's praise is cut off. Other reported wordings say that a matter not begun with God's remembrance is cut off, and that any serious matter not begun with God's name is cut off. Ibn al-Athīr explains “cut off” (abtar) as “severed” (aqṭaʿ), from the notion of cutting. The same word describes a certain deficient sermon of Ziyād, which was reportedly so named because God was not mentioned in it and no blessing was invoked upon the Prophet.

### Editorial/source qualification

Translations of reported traditions; authenticity and chains NOT checked. Reader should not treat these as authenticated prophetic quotations.

<a id="hwn-008"></a>
## Note 8 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [128](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/128) · [129](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/129). **Reported voice:** Muḥammad ʿAlī (signed marginal gloss; sectarian claim is authorial, not editorial). **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

عطف على الضمير المجرور في عليه

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The expression is coordinated with the pronoun governed by the preposition in “upon him” (ʿalayhi), without repeating that preposition. This is permitted by the Kufan grammarians, and also by al-Akhfash, Yūnus and Ibn Mālik: according to them it is not obligatory to repeat the governing preposition. The Basrans, however, require its repetition. They argue that the attachment of a pronoun in the genitive to its preposition is even tighter than the attachment of a nominative pronoun to its verbal governor. Just as coordination with the latter is forbidden, since the pronoun has become virtually part of the word, so they forbid it here.

The marginal commentator then introduces a contentious sectarian explanation of why the author might have omitted the additional word ʿalā (“upon”). He suggests that the omission was meant to affront the ʿāmma, because a report had circulated that the khāṣṣa omit ʿalā when coordinating mention of the Prophet's family with mention of the Prophet. According to the report ascribed to the Prophet, whoever separates him from his family “by ʿAlī/ʿalā” will not obtain his intercession. The commentator says that the opposing party regards the insertion of ʿalā as objectionable.

But, he continues, this would be a false allegation against the khāṣṣa, because ʿalā does occur repeatedly when the family are coordinated with the Prophet in supplications transmitted from their Imams. Whoever examines those prayers can see this.

He therefore offers two possibilities. Either the alleged report is fabricated and must be rejected; or its disputed words are to be vocalised with a kasra under the lām and a doubled yāʾ, giving the proper name ʿAlī rather than the preposition ʿalā. On that second construal the meaning would be: whoever separates the Prophet from members of his family, from al-Ḥasan and al-Ḥusayn through the awaited Ḥujja, *by invoking ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭālib*—that is, by declaring them not to be the Prophet's family because they descend through a daughter—would be denied intercession. The commentator adduces a line of verse to express the genealogical objection: “The sons of our sons are our sons; our daughters' sons belong to men more remote.” If one reasoned that way, one would call those descendants the family of ʿAlī rather than the family of the Prophet. Reflect.

These are the speaker's grammatical, transmitted, and confessional claims. They do not constitute independent authentication of the alleged prophetic report, and the translation does not endorse the polemical inference.

### Editorial/source qualification

Stage105 translates the former summary's omitted sectarian polemic and poetic citation. Preserve attribution to the glossator; reported tradition is not source-authenticated.

<a id="hwn-009"></a>
## Note 9 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [129](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/129) · [130](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/130) · [131](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/131). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī (signed at Q131). **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

في حديث الابتداء بالتسمية والتحميد اشكالين مشهورين

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On the words “If you ask, the report concerning the beginning is transmitted ...”: know that two familiar difficulties arise from the traditions commanding that important undertakings begin with the divine name and with praise.

The first difficulty is that the basmala and the praise formula are themselves important matters. The expression “a matter of consequence” (dhū bāl) can mean something of standing or status, as in Qurʾānic questions about former generations and the women who cut their hands; alternatively bāl can be associated with the heart, so that the term means whatever occurs to and occupies one's attention. If the rule includes either introductory formula itself, that formula must begin with another formula of the same kind; the second must begin with a third, and so forth. The procedure must either return to what started it, producing circularity, or continue without end, producing an infinite regress. Requiring one formula to start with the other creates the same pair of alternatives.

The second difficulty is that applying one of the two traditions seems to cancel the other. Either one begins with the basmala, thereby omitting the initial praise commanded by the other report, or begins with praise, omitting the initial naming of God. This is the difficulty to which the author is presently referring.

One answer to the first difficulty is that everything that exists through another ultimately depends on what exists through itself. This is illustrated by the transmitted saying about the divine will—God created the will through itself and other things through the will—and by the psychological illustration that the things one brings about are produced through one's will whereas the will itself arises without another prior willing, according to the view of the upholders of divine justice. Similarly, the other affairs begin with the basmala or praise, while the opening formulas begin through themselves.

Another answer is that the general statement is restricted by a contextual indication: to begin *one thing with another* already implies a distinction between them. The same restriction is invoked when it is said that God creates every thing and that every thing depends on God: the intended meaning is everything other than God. It is common to restrict a general expression, hence the saying that scarcely any general statement escapes qualification. These two answers, the glossator observes, remove only the first of the alleged consequences; the other difficulty still remains.

The preferable answer construes “a matter of consequence” in the two reports not as everything that is objectively important, but as the act or purpose intended for its own sake. Neither introductory formula is the independent undertaking intended for its own sake, although both are important in reality. Thus neither falls under the subject of the command in the problematic sense.

As for the second difficulty, the commentator's answer depends on three kinds of beginning. (1) A **real** beginning precedes without itself being preceded; another definition says that nothing precedes it at all. (2) A **relative** beginning comes before the thing principally intended, even if it comes after some other thing; a broader definition treats it as first in relation to one item though preceded in relation to another. The first definition is narrower. (3) A **customary** beginning is whatever ordinary usage regards as part of the beginning whether or not something precedes it; it can extend from the commencement of composing the book to the moment when the main intended discussion begins. The narrow relative beginning and the customary one may differ only in the standpoint from which we consider them.

Each report admits these three readings, giving nine possible pairings. Three are both sound and counted as relevant; those are the readings invoked by the primary commentator. Three are sound but omitted as not adopted: reading both beginnings relatively; reading the basmala relatively and the praise customarily; and conversely reading the basmala customarily and the praise relatively. The remaining three are unsound: these read the praise as the *real* beginning while interpreting the basmala by any of the three definitions, contrary to the actual custom under consideration.

The grounds of validity and invalidity follow from the definitions, but why should a logically valid pair be counted or left out? The commentator considers two suggested criteria: either one of the beginnings is real, as in the first two recognised possibilities, or both expressions receive a beginning of the same kind, as in the last. The two omitted mixed cases have neither property; however, the excluded relative-relative case plainly satisfies the second. Either it should be counted too or the accepted customary-customary case should also be disallowed. Otherwise the distinction is arbitrary. One might reply only that customary use is common in this discussion whereas specifically relative use is not.

He cautions that being “counted” or “uncounted” presupposes being *valid*. An invalid case cannot become valid just because it happens to share both criteria. Moreover the whole scheme assumes the current convention of placing the basmala before the praise. Reverse the order and both the validity and the relevance of some combinations change. Care is needed not to confuse the cases.

Two further replies to the original difficulty are mentioned. One might say that praise means displaying God's beautiful attributes, whether by the word ḥamd or otherwise, so that the basmala itself has an aspect of praising and satisfies both reports. Or one might take the two reports as jointly prohibiting the absence of *both*: a significant undertaking lacking either the basmala or praise is defective, and a truly initial use of either one suffices. The basmala would still be preferable because it also contains praise and alludes to the assistance of God's supreme name in action.

Both proposals, he objects, strain the apparent wording. The second quite clearly does. The first conflicts with ordinary linguistic usage: when someone utters only the basmala, people do not normally say that he has uttered praise. It is for this reason that the primary commentator did not choose these replies.

### Editorial/source qualification

Stage105 translates the remaining Q129–131 numbered note in full first pass; report/hadith are quoted arguments, not authenticated evidence. Nine-case classification preserved including commentator's objection.

## Note 10 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [131](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/131). **Reported voice:** ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

الثناء بالمد هو الذكر بالخير

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Thanāʾ (with a long vowel) means speaking well of someone or mentioning that person favourably. It is not used for speaking ill, except by a special interpretive device, such as assimilating one expression to another for rhetorical parallelism (mushākala).

### Editorial/source qualification

Short lexical note.

## Note 11 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [131](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/131). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله على الجميل احتراز عن الذم والهجاء

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

His phrase “for what is beautiful” serves to exclude blame and satire if thanāʾ is understood broadly enough to embrace both commendation and condemnation, as the Qāmūs states. If one instead confines thanāʾ to commendation, the phrase merely makes explicit the intended reality and meaning. An objection may be made: a tyrant might be exalted for unjustly plundering property and killing heroes; is that not praise despite being directed to what is not beautiful? Should “beautiful” not be replaced by “action”? We deny that such commendation is properly called ḥamd. Even if this is conceded, “beautiful” can include what is beautiful in the opinion of the praiser: a person may regard an action as fine even if others—or reality itself—regard it as reprehensible. It can also be beautiful merely in outward appearance.

### Editorial/source qualification

The note distinguishes objective, subjective, and apparent beauty; it does not establish that an intrinsically vicious act is morally good.

---

<a id="hwn-012"></a>
## Note 12 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [131](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/131) · [132](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/132). **Reported voice:** Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله الاختياري وصف به ليخرج عن الحد المدح

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Concerning the expression “voluntary”: the qualifier was included so that madḥ (commendation) would fall outside the definition of ḥamd (praise), since commendation extends both to beautiful things that are chosen and to those that are not chosen. One may say, “I commended him for the beauty of his cheek and the elegance of his stature,” whereas one would not normally say, “I praised him [ḥamidtuhu] for that.” This distinction is necessary only if madḥ is broader than ḥamd. If the two are treated as synonymous, as appears from al-Zamakhsharī's formulation “ḥamd and madḥ are two brothers,” the qualifier should instead be abandoned as he abandoned it.

An objection: the author himself leaves out “voluntary” in his commentary on the Talkhīṣ when he says that ḥamd is commendation with the tongue for what is beautiful, whether the excellence be personal virtues (faḍāʾil) or benefits extended to others (fawāḍil). Does he not therefore also regard the terms as synonymous?

I reply: no. He treats “beautiful” as modifying a suppressed noun: the intended expression is “for a beautiful *action*.” That amounts to saying “for a beautiful voluntary thing,” because, as they explicitly explain, an act in the relevant sense is something done by choice.

A further objection comes from kalām. God's eternal attributes are not chosen in the sense of being newly acquired; otherwise their acquisition would entail their origination. Yet those who laud Him for His eternal attributes are, by agreement, said to praise Him. Does the voluntary condition not exclude this?

I reply, assuming that ḥamd in this instance is literal, that the eternal attributes may be treated analogically as voluntary acts insofar as God independently possesses and entails those attributes, as He is independent in His voluntary acts. Alternatively, the praise that appears to be directed to the eternal attributes is in truth directed to the voluntary acts of which those attributes are principles. Thus the grammatical or immediate object of praise and its ultimate object need not be identical. Reflect.

### Editorial/source qualification

Stage105 completes numbered note 12 ending at Q132 '(محمد علي)'. The following al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī and al-Karmī passages are later separately attributed quotations, NOT note 12.

<a id="hwn-015"></a>
## Note 15 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [133](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/133) · [134](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/134). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

والله علم على الأصح

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On the words “Allāh is, according to the sounder view, a proper name”: as human minds have been bewildered by the divine essence and attributes, so they have disputed the word by which He is designated. Is it Arabic, Hebrew, or Syriac? Is it a noun or an adjective? Derived or primitive? A proper name or a general expression? Each group has advanced different proofs. Rather than multiplying reports to the point of weariness, we shall state the preferred account briefly.

The word Allāh originally derives, on this account, from ilāh, on the morphological pattern fiʿāl in the sense of the thing to which an action is done: maʾlūh, the one worshipped, just as kitāb can refer to the thing written. Its original hamza was then dropped, and the definite article was substituted as compensation. On that account it became permissible to address Him by the name directly, without an intervening particle, and often to pronounce its initial hamza as a hamzat qaṭʿ in calling upon Him. This treatment would not ordinarily be permitted for common nouns or relative expressions bearing an ordinary article.

Why, then, is the hamza not pronounced independently in other contexts? The name still retains something of its definite force, for which liaison is suitable. In direct address that aspect recedes, leaving the article as compensatory and permitting the divine name to be uttered with the vocative yā. The special pronunciation also conveys honour from the outset: the word has departed from its former construction, and the article has become almost a constituent part of it.

Al-Jawharī offers an alternative: the hamza was omitted *after* adding the article, simply because of the word's frequency, not because the article compensates for it. People do, after all, say al-ilāh, combining the two; if the article existed solely as compensation, that ought not to be possible. Nor does the independently pronounced hamza in vocative use prove substitution, for it might be a mark of magnification. The present glossator records this argument but says that it calls for examination.

The designation then becomes a proper name of the *particular* necessarily existent being who possesses every attribute of perfection. The author says in his Sharḥ al-Talkhīṣ that those who treat “Allāh” merely as the concept “necessary through itself” or “entitled to worship” miss the point. Both descriptions are universal concepts, even if they happen to have only one actual instance, whereas a proper name has a particular signification.

Consider the confession “There is no god but Allāh.” It is agreed to declare unity without requiring a separately supplied contextual identification. If “Allāh” named merely the concept “rightful object of worship” or “necessary through itself,” rather than the individual being who alone instantiates it, the formulation would not by itself exclude multiple instances: a universal concept, considered in itself, allows plurality. Furthermore ilāh in the confession cannot mean simply “whatever is worshipped,” since many false objects are worshipped and the statement would then be untrue. Nor can “Allāh” simply mean the same universal notion “that which deserves worship,” for the exception would then appear to exclude an item from itself. The proper analysis takes ilāh as “one rightfully deserving worship” and Allāh as the proper name of the actual individual in whom that description is realised. The meaning is that nobody in existence deserves worship except the one who created the world.

A difficulty remains: to assign a proper name to a thing presupposes apprehending that thing, but the divine essence cannot be comprehended. How can the name therefore be a proper name for Him? And if it is a proper name, why is “One” informative when predicated of it in the Qurʾānic words “Say: He is Allāh, One”? A person already knows that an individual denoted by a proper name is one; one does not ordinarily say “Zayd is one.”

The reply to the first difficulty is that God's distinction from all else can certainly be apprehended through His beautiful attributes. What is impossible is grasping the very innermost reality of His essence and attributes. Such exhaustive comprehension is not required when establishing a proper name; it is enough to conceive its bearer as distinct from everything else. Many ordinary proper names are assigned under the same limitation.

Even if one granted that a human being could not institute this proper name, the stronger universal conclusion would not follow. One might hold that God Himself instituted the name and taught it to others through inspiration, revelation, or created sounds, as some investigators maintain concerning the original institutor of language generally or of this name in particular.

Nor is the alleged redundancy of predicating “One” accepted. It depends on interpreting huwa in “Say: He is Allāh, One” as an impersonal pronoun of the affair, with “Allāh is one” forming the explicative subject-predicate sentence. That is not obligatory. The pronoun can refer to the Lord whom the interlocutors had asked the Prophet to describe. The verse then says, in effect: “He—my Lord, to whom I call you—is Allāh, One.” “One” can be taken as an apposition or a second predicate. Even if the impersonal construction be allowed, “One” need not mean merely the numerical count of one rather than two. It can signify that He is not divided into parts, or that He is first, or that He has no equal or counterpart. Under each meaning the predication contributes knowledge.

### Editorial/source qualification

Stage105 replaces prior abbreviated note 15 with a consecutive first-pass translation of Q133–134. The immediately following shorter notes 16–18 and late modern al-Karmī quotation retain their separate provenance.

## Note 16 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [134](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/134). **Reported voice:** Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

الذات قد يطلق ويراد بها حقيقة الشئ

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Know that dhāt may be used to mean the reality or essence of a thing. It may also mean what stands opposite to an attribute or description; that is the intended sense here. In this latter usage it can function like nafs (“self”) or shayʾ (“thing”), so it may be treated as grammatically feminine or masculine. Al-Akhfash, commenting on the Qurʾānic phrase “set right what is between you,” explains the feminine dhāt by observing that some things are conventionally designated by feminine nouns and others by masculine ones: “house” is feminine, while “wall” is masculine.

### Editorial/source qualification

Sex/gender here concerns grammatical gender, not human characteristics.

## Note 17 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [134](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/134). **Reported voice:** Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله الواجب الوجود قال بعض المحققين

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Concerning the expression “the necessary existent,” one careful marginal commentator says that “through itself” should be understood, since it is the immediate meaning when the expression is unqualified; necessity through another belongs to a possible being, which neither bears the name Allāh nor deserves the unrestricted praise at issue. The present glossator questions this explanation: the expression “the necessary” when used absolutely already denotes what is necessary through itself. A possible being is not described as necessary without qualification, only as “necessary through another” when the relevant dependence is expressly stated. Consequently there is no need to posit an omitted phrase or defend it by appealing to what first comes to mind.

### Editorial/source qualification

Position reported and rejected by this glossator; avoid treating the statement as an uncontested consensus across metaphysical traditions.

## Note 20 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [135](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/135). **Reported voice:** Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

من حيث هو مستجمع لجميع الصفات الكمالية

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

This means “insofar as He possesses every attribute of perfection.” When a ruling is connected with a description in an address meant to persuade, that connection suggests that the underlying characteristic gives the reason for the ruling, at least with the help of contextual clues. For example, when someone says “Honour the scholar,” the context indicates that it is knowledge which provides the reason for honouring the person. So too the divine possession of all perfections supplies the reason for unrestricted praise.

### Editorial/source qualification

Distinguishes descriptive aspect as causal ground from mere association; a rhetorical-logic claim, not a historical proof of efficient causality.

## Note 22 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [135](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/135). **Reported voice:** Mullā Jalāl al-Dīn (reported as source at close). **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قيل الهداية الدلالة على ما يوصل

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

It is said that guidance (hidāya) is indicating something that leads to the desired objective. Others say instead that it is an indication which actually brings the person to the goal. The first definition has been preferred, while the second has been challenged by the Qurʾānic statement that Thamūd were guided yet preferred blindness to guidance. On the other hand, the first definition has been challenged by the statement to the Prophet that he cannot guide whomever he loves. The possibility of figurative usage is available on either side, so neither party can claim it exclusively. There is room to question whether the disputed usage must be excluded from the proposed meaning. A marginal comment on al-Kashshāf observes that “guide” may take its object directly, with ilā, or with lām; on the first construction it can denote bringing someone to the destination, and with the prepositions it may denote showing the road.

### Editorial/source qualification

First-pass complete reported note 22, preserving the two competing definitions and reciprocal Qurʾānic counterexamples. Sources cited in the gloss still require independent reference verification.

<a id="hwn-024"></a>
## Note 24 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [135](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/135). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله : « اى الايصال » : اختلف العلماء في ان ما بعد « اى » التفسيرية هل هو عطف بيان لما قبلها أو عطف نسق ؟ والجمهور على الأول وصاحب المفتاح ومن تبعه على الثّاني . قال المصنف في شرح التلخيص : « ووقوعها تفسيرا للضمير المجرور من غير إعادة الجار وللضّمير المتصل المرفوع من غير تأكيد أو فصل يقوى مذهب الجمهور » . وفيه ان تلك الخصوصية وان كانت تقويه ، لكن مجرد وقوعها تفسيرا للضمير يقوى مذهب « السكاكى » بناء على ما نص عليه النّحويون : من أن عطف البيان في الجوامد بمنزلة النعت في المشتقات . فكما ان الضمير لا ينعت ، كذلك لا يعطف عليه عطف بيان . ولذا عاب ابن هشام على الزمخشري في تجويزه في قوله تعالى : « ما قُلْتُ لَهُمْ إِلَّا ما أَمَرْتَنِي بِهِ أَنِ اعْبُدُوا اللَّهَ رَبِّي وَرَبَّكُمْ . . . » ( سورة مائدة آية 117 ) ان يكون « ان » مصدرية وهي وصلتها عطف بيان على الهاء في به .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On the expression “that is, bringing [someone] to the goal”: Scholars have disagreed about whether the words coming after explanatory *ayy* are an appositional clarification (*ʿaṭf bayān*) of what precedes or are connected by ordinary coordination (*ʿaṭf nasaq*). The majority adopt the first position; the author of the *Miftāḥ* (al-Sakkākī) and his followers adopt the second.

Al-Taftāzānī says in his commentary on the *Talkhīṣ*: the fact that explanatory *ayy* can explicate a pronoun governed by a preposition without the preposition being repeated, or a directly attached nominative pronoun without reinforcement or separation, supports the majority's position.

The glossator objects: this special grammatical behaviour may indeed support that position, but the very fact that it explains a pronoun supports al-Sakkākī instead. Grammarians have explicitly said that appositional clarification of a nonderived noun occupies the role that an adjective occupies with a derived expression. Just as a pronoun does not normally receive an adjectival modifier, it should not receive *ʿaṭf bayān* either.

That is why Ibn Hishām criticised al-Zamakhsharī for allowing, in the Qurʾānic statement “I told them only what You commanded me: worship God, my Lord and your Lord” (Q 5:117), the infinitival *an* and its following clause to be taken as appositional clarification of the *-hi* in *bihi*. The example shows the precise issue: explaining a pronominal expression is not automatically the same grammatical operation as attaching an appositional noun to it.

### Editorial/source qualification

First-pass complete English and searchable-print Arabic for the entirety of numbered note 24; the source's exact diacritics and marginal placement have not been photo-checked.

<a id="hwn-026"></a>
## Note 26 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [135](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/135) · [136](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/136). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm, citing Quṭb al-Dīn al-Shīrāzī and al-Dawwānī. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله وقيل هي إراءة الطريق : الطريق على فعيل بمعنى السبيل يساوى فيه المذكر والمؤنث وجمعه « الطّرق » وجمع الجمع « الطرقات » بضمتين ثم القائل هو قطب الدين حيث قال في شرح المطالع : الهداية الدلالة على ما يوصل إلى المطلوب ، ورجحه بعض المحققين ونقضه « الفاضل الدواني » بما نقض به المحشى . واعلم أن تعريفها بوجدان ما يوصل إلى المطلوب باطل قطعا . لان ذلك الوجدان هو الاهتداء لا الهداية . والأقوى ان من وجد المطالب الكمالية ولم يدل غيره عليها يقال : هو مهتد ولا يقال : هو هاد .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On the statement “And it is said that guidance is showing the road”: the word *ṭarīq*, on the morphological pattern *faʿīl*, means a way or path (*sabīl*) and is used alike for masculine and feminine referents. Its plural is *ṭuruq*; a plural of that plural is *ṭuruqāt*, with ḍamma on the relevant letters.

The person who gives the stated definition is Quṭb al-Dīn al-Shīrāzī. In his commentary on the *Maṭāliʿ* he says: “Guidance is indicating what leads to the sought objective.” Some investigators preferred this definition, while the distinguished al-Dawwānī objected to it on the very ground raised by the marginal commentator.

Know, moreover, that defining guidance as *finding* what leads to the objective is plainly wrong. That finding constitutes *being guided* (*ihtidāʾ*), not *guiding* (*hidāya*). The better distinction is that someone who has discovered the objects of perfection but has not pointed them out to anyone else is described as *guided* (*muhtadin*), not as a *guide* (*hādin*). The act of communicating direction must not be confused with the subject's possession of the right knowledge.

### Editorial/source qualification

Note 26 crosses the printed Q135/Q136 seam; first-pass complete both sides, attribution from terminal (عبد الرحيم).

<a id="hwn-027"></a>
## Note 27 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [136](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/136). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله : والأول منقوض بقوله تعالى : « وَأَمَّا ثَمُودُ فَهَدَيْناهُمْ . . . » هو قبيلة صالح ( ع ) « فهديناهم » اى : دللنا هم على طريقي الضلالة والرشد وبيّنا لهم سبيل الخير والشر « فاستحبوا العمى على الهدى » فاختاروا الكفر على الايمان . لا يقال : لا يرد النقض بهذه الآية الشريفة على المعنى الأول ، لجواز كون الهداية فيها مستعملة بالمعنى الثاني مجازا مع كونها حقيقة في المعنى الأول . لأنا نقول : هذا الكلام مشترك الورود ، فللقائل الثاني ان يدعى مثل ذلك في الآية الثانية .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On the assertion that the first interpretation is refuted by the Qurʾānic words “As for Thamūd, We guided them”: Thamūd were the people of Ṣāliḥ. “We guided them” means that We showed them the ways of error and rectitude and made clear the paths of good and evil. They nevertheless “preferred blindness to guidance,” that is, they chose unbelief over belief.

One must not object that the verse fails to refute the first proposed sense because *guidance* might be used here figuratively in the second sense while retaining its original literal meaning in the first. We reply: precisely the same manoeuvre is available to the advocate of the rival definition, who could make an equivalent figurative-usage claim about the other verse. The mere possibility of *majāz* does not decide which definition is basic.

### Editorial/source qualification

Cross-references Q 41:17, report of a commentator's semantic arguments, not a doctrinal adjudication.

<a id="hwn-028"></a>
## Note 28 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [136](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/136). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

بيان طريق الانتقاض بالآية ، وحاصله انّه : لو كان معنى الهداية الايصال إلى الحق لكان معنى الآية ح : ان ثمود اوصلناهم إلى الحق فاستحبوا الضلالة على الرشاد لاستلزام الايصال الوصول فان المراد من الايصال ، الايصال بالفعل لا بالقوة ولا المطلق حتى يقال إنه : لا يستلزم الوصول ، إذ الأول هو معنى إراءة الطريق بعينه والثاني لا ينافيه وليس بمراد قطعا ، إذ الضلالة لا يتصور بعد الرشاد والوصول إلى الحق ، بل المقصود كما صرح به المفسرون : ان ثمود عرفنا هم الحق وبينّاه لهم ودعوناهم اليه فاستحبوا العمى والضلالة على الهدى والرشاد وهم يعرفون . لا يقال : انا لا نسلم ان الضلالة لا يتصور بعد الوصول ، لأنا نرى بالعيان خلاف ذلك فان بعض المؤمنين قد يصير مرتدا باغواء الشيطان . لأنا نقول : ان هذا الشخص وان كان بحسب الظاهر مؤمنا وأصلا إلى الحق يجرى عليه احكام الايمان ، لكن ارتداده بعد ، يصير كاشفا عن عدم وصوله اليه في الواقع ونفس الامر فان المؤمن الحقيقي لا يتطرق اليه الشيطان وشبهاته كما هو ظاهر . قال تعالى : « وَمَنْ يَهْدِ اللَّهُ فَما لَهُ مِنْ مُضِلٍّ » . ثم لا يخفى انه لا يمكن ان يقال : ان الهداية استعملت في الآية وأمثالها في المعنى الثاني على سبيل التجوّز وهذا لا ينافي في كونها موضوعة للمعنى الأول في أصل الوضع لان هذا ليس بأولى من أن يقال : انّها وضعت في أصل الوضع للمعنى الثاني واستعملت في قوله تعالى : « إِنَّكَ لا تَهْدِي مَنْ أَحْبَبْتَ » في المعنى الأول مجازا فتامّل .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The following explains how the verse yields an objection. If *guidance* meant actually bringing someone to the truth, the verse about Thamūd would say, in effect: “We brought them to the truth, but they preferred error to rectitude.” Bringing someone to the goal entails their arrival. The disputed sense of “bringing” is actual attainment, not a mere capacity to bring or some indeterminate relationship to arrival. The first weaker sense would collapse into showing the road; the second does not exclude that sense and is not what the stronger definition intends.

Yet the account of Thamūd's choice presupposes that they had not reached truth in this decisive sense. As the exegetes explain, God made the truth known to them, explained it, and invited them towards it; despite recognising it, they preferred blindness and misguidance to rectitude.

Someone might object that misguidance *can* follow real attainment: we can observe believers later becoming apostates through satanic deception. The glossator responds by distinguishing outwardly recognised belief from a purportedly genuine inner arrival. Although legal rulings of faith applied to the apostate beforehand, the later apostasy is, on his account, evidence that the person had not truly attained the truth inwardly. He invokes the Qurʾānic saying that none can lead astray whom God guides. This is the glossator's theological premise in the argument, not an empirically established law about the psychological impossibility of conversion.

Finally, one cannot simply say that the verse about Thamūd uses “guidance” figuratively in the weaker sense while the literal, originally instituted meaning is actual arrival. The opposite claim is equally available: that the original literal meaning is showing the road, whereas “You do not guide those whom you love” uses the word figuratively in the stronger sense. Without independent evidence, appeal to figurative usage merely shifts the question.

### Editorial/source qualification

The glossator's exclusion of apostasy after genuine attainment is explicitly attributed, not treated as this edition's theological assertion. Distinguishes actual, potential and generic 'causing to reach'.

---

<a id="hwn-029"></a>
## Note 29 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [136](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/136) · [137](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/137). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

يعنى ان معنى الآية الشريفة : انك يا محمد ( ص ) لا تقدر ان تدخل من أحببت هدايته في الإسلام ولكن اللّه يقدر على ذلك فيهدى من يشاء إلى الإسلام ويدخله فيه بلطفه وقيل بالاجبار ، فالهداية فيها بمعنى الايصال قطعا ، وكيف لا ؟ وكان شأن النبي ( ص ) إراءة الطريق والدعوة إلى الحق ، قال تعالى : « وَإِنَّكَ لَتَهْدِي إِلى صِراطٍ مُسْتَقِيمٍ » فح ينتقض القول الثاني بها وذلك ظاهر . ثم لا يخفى انه : يمكن ان يقال : ان الآية نزلت على طريقة قوله تعالى : « وَما رَمَيْتَ إِذْ رَمَيْتَ وَلكِنَّ اللَّهَ رَمى » فكما جعل الرمي الصادر عن النبي ( ص ) كأنه غير صادر عنه بل عن اللّه تبارك وتعالى لكون اثره خارجا عن طوق البشر فقيل : وما رميت إذ رميت ، فكذلك هنا جعل الهداية الصادرة عنه ( ص ) كانّها غير صادرة عنه ( ص ) بل عن اللّه تبارك وتعالى . فالأولى ان يذكر النقض بقوله تعالى : « . . . وَاللَّهُ لا يَهْدِي الْقَوْمَ الظَّالِمِينَ » فتأمل .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

This means that the Qurʾānic address “You do not guide whom you love” says to Muḥammad: you cannot yourself bring into Islam everyone whose guidance you desire. God, however, can do so, guiding whom He wills and causing that person to enter Islam through His gracious aid—although another view describes this as compulsion. In this passage, then, *guidance* must signify actual conveyance to the goal rather than simply pointing it out. The Prophet's own appointed function included showing the road and summoning people to truth. The Qurʾān says of him, “You surely guide to a straight path”; thus the verse denying that he guides would refute the purely demonstrative definition if the sense were identical.

Yet the glossator adds a caution. Perhaps the verse is expressed in the manner of “You did not throw when you threw, but God threw.” Although the Prophet did perform the outward act of throwing, the effect surpassed human capacity and was therefore attributed decisively to God. Similarly, the guidance carried out through the Prophet may be represented as belonging to God because its ultimate efficacy is divine. If that is the reading, the apparent denial of guidance to the Prophet is not a secure independent proof for one lexical definition.

For this reason it might be better to press the objection using the Qurʾānic expression “God does not guide the wrongdoing people.” The glossator ends by inviting reflection; he has not shown that every occurrence of the verb possesses the same fixed sense.

### Editorial/source qualification

The note spans Q136→Q137; competing theological causes (luṭf/ijbār) explicitly reported, not harmonised as if one view.

<a id="hwn-030"></a>
## Note 30 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [137](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/137). **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb p.7 (quoted). **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله فان النبي كان شأنه إراءة الطريق : طبعا من مقام نبوته فكيف يصدق في حقه « انك لا تهدى من أحببت » ؟ اى : لا ترى الطريق لمن تحب ، نعم تصح الآية في حقه إذا كان معناه لا توصل إلى الحق من أحببت ايصاله . فإنه قد تكون هناك موانع تقف بك في مقام ايصالك لمحبوبك إلى صميم الحق في وسط الطريق وتعجزك عن الوصول . فالآية الأولى نقض على المعنى الأول والآية الثانية نقض على المعنى الثاني وإذا كان لفظ الهداية مشتركا بين الايصال والاراءة يكون معنى « فهديناهم » في الآية الأولى : أريناهم الطريق الموصل إلى الحق فلم يسلكوه وبقوا تائهين تعمدا ويكون معنى « لا تهدى من أحببت » لا توصل من أحببت إلى الحق وان كنت تقدر على الاراءة وتقوم بواجبها مع كافة الناس .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On the statement “For the Prophet's role was to show the road”: that belongs naturally to his very office as prophet. How, then, could “You do not guide whom you love” truly mean “You do not show the road to someone you love”? The Prophet does show it. The verse is intelligible if it instead means: “You cannot bring into the truth everyone whom you wish to bring there,” for impediments may arise along the way and prevent you from bringing the person to the innermost reality of the truth.

Thus the earlier verse about Thamūd is an objection to the definition of guidance exclusively as actual conveyance, while the later verse is an objection to the definition exclusively as showing. If the word has both uses, one can read “We guided them” in the first as “We showed them the road that leads to the truth, but they deliberately refused to follow it and remained lost,” while “You do not guide whom you love” means “You do not ensure that the person arrives,” even though you are capable of showing the road and fulfil your duty to show it to all people.

### Editorial/source qualification

Source voice labelled al-Taqrīb p.7; page-level inclusion does not establish authorship by Yazdī or all the later glossators.

<a id="hwn-031"></a>
## Note 31 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [137](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/137?lang=ar) · [138](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/138?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله والذي يفهم من المصنف ( ره ) : قال المصنف في تلك الحاشية : لا كلام في مجىء هديته الطريق وهديته للطريق وهديته إلى الطريق ، وقد يفرق بينها بان معنى الأول : الاذهاب إلى المقصد والايصال اليه ولهذا يسند إلى اللّه خاصة ومعنى الثاني : الدلالة وإراءة الطريق ، فيسند إلى النبي ( ص ) مثل انك لتهدى إلى صراط مستقيم ، وإلى القرآن مثل ان هذا القرآن يهدى للتي هي أقوم . انتهى . وأقول : ما ذكره من الفرق مردود بقوله تعالى : « إِنَّا هَدَيْناهُ السَّبِيلَ إِمَّا شاكِراً وَإِمَّا كَفُوراً » لما روى عن الصادق ( ع ) : ان المعنى : عرّفناه اما آخذا واما تاركا ولان الكفران والضلالة لا يتصور بعد الوصول والهداية كما سبق اليه الإشارة . واما قوله تعالى : « وَهَدَيْناهُ النَّجْدَيْنِ » فقيل : انه أيضا يكون ردا ، لأنه ورد في معرض الامتنان ولا يمن بالايصال إلى الشر ، فان المراد من النجدين طريق الخير وطريق الشر كما وردت عليه الرواية . والحق انه لا رد فيه لجواز ان يقال : ان المراد من النجدين الثديان كما يدل عليه ما قبله : « ألم نجعل له عينين ولسانا وشفتين » . وورد عليه أيضا الرواية . وقوله انها بالاستعمال الأول يخص بالله تعالى منقوض بقوله تعالى حكاية عن إبراهيم ( ع ) : « فَاتَّبِعْنِي أَهْدِكَ صِراطاً سَوِيًّا » وبقوله تعالى : « وَقالَ الَّذِي آمَنَ يا قَوْمِ اتَّبِعُونِ أَهْدِكُمْ سَبِيلَ الرَّشادِ » وبقوله تعالى : « قالَ فِرْعَوْنُ ما أُرِيكُمْ إِلَّا ما أَرى وَما أَهْدِيكُمْ إِلَّا سَبِيلَ الرَّشادِ » . ودعوى ان أمثال ذلك من قبيل قوله تعالى : « وَاخْتارَ مُوسى قَوْمَهُ . . . » مردودة ، بكونه على خلاف الأصل ولو سلم فيلدعى ذلك في جميع المواضع كما ادعاه الزمخشري ، فلا يكون الهداية على هذا للايصال أصلا فافهم . فالحق : ان الهداية هي الدلالة بلطف مطلقا سواء كانت دلالة موصلة إلى المطلوب أم دلالة موصلة على ما يوصل إلى المطلوب وسواء كانت متعدية إلى المفعول الثاني بنفسه أم بغيره كما يدل عليه السابقة منا ومن المحشى وقد نص على ذلك جمع من المحققين فاحفظ .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On the words “What is understood from the author's statement”: the author said in that gloss that there is no doubt about the three constructions “I guided him the road,” “I guided him to the road” with lām, and “I guided him toward the road” with ilā. One may distinguish them by saying that the first means taking someone to the destination and successfully bringing him there, hence is applied particularly to God. The second and third signify indication or showing the road, and can accordingly be applied to the Prophet—as in “You guide to a straight path”—and to the Qurʾān, as in “This Qurʾān guides to what is most upright.” End of his quotation.

I say: this contrast is rejected by “We guided him the road, whether grateful or ungrateful” (76:3). It has been reported from al-Ṣādiq that this means “We made the road known to him, whether he accepted it or left it.” One could not be ungrateful and astray after having already been brought to truth in the relevant stronger sense.

Some have also used “We guided him the two high roads” (90:10) as an objection: this is in a context of divine favour, whereas taking someone all the way to evil could not be a benefit; the two high roads have been interpreted as the paths of good and evil. But this second objection is not conclusive, since the two high roads could instead mean the two breasts, as suggested by the preceding reference to the person's eyes, tongue and lips; a report also gives that interpretation.

The contention that the first grammatical construction is reserved to God is also challenged by reported speech from Abraham, “Follow me; I shall guide you a straight path” (19:43), from the believer who says “Follow me; I shall guide you the way of right direction” (40:38), and even from Pharaoh, “I guide you only to the way of right direction” (40:29). To force all of these into exceptional constructions like “Moses chose his people” would be contrary to the usual reading. Even if such reanalysis were allowed, one could apply it to every example, as al-Zamakhsharī did, leaving no actual use of guidance as successful delivery.

The sounder account, I say, is that guidance means indicating something with kindness, whether the indication actually brings someone to the goal or directs him to what leads there, and whether its second object follows without a preposition or through a preposition. Both preceding objections and the earlier gloss support this broader sense, as do several investigators.

### Editorial/source qualification

Note physically crosses Q137 to Q138. Lexical conclusion is the glossator's own proposal; alternative Qurʾānic interpretations remain attributed, not independently certified.

<a id="hwn-032"></a>
## Note 32 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [138](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/138?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

اى : وحين إذ كان الهداية لفظا مشتركا بين المعنيين المذكورين : الايصال والاراءة ، يظهر اندفاع النقضين لأنه يقال : انها في الآية الأولى للارائة والمفعول الثاني المحذوف مقدر مع إلى أو اللام وفي الآية الثانية للايصال والمفعول الثاني مقدر بدونهما . ثم الفرق بين الدفع والرفع هو : ان الأول يقال لاعدام الشئ قبل مجيئه والثاني لإعدامه بعد مجيئه عكس الوضع .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

If “guidance” is a word shared by the two aforementioned meanings—bringing someone to the goal and showing the road—both counterexamples are removed. In the Thamūd verse guidance can denote showing, with the omitted second object understood as governed by ilā or lām. In “You do not guide whom you love” it denotes actual delivery, with the omitted second object understood without either particle.

The difference between dafʿ and rafʿ is this: the first is used of preventing something before it comes into existence, and the second of removing it after it has appeared. They are opposed in this respect to waḍʿ, the placing or establishment of a thing.

### Editorial/source qualification

The semantic contrast dafʿ/rafʿ is part of the note even though it changes subject.

<a id="hwn-033"></a>
## Note 33 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [138](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/138?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله نحو اهدنا الصراط المستقيم : السراط بالسين : الجادة من سرط الشئ إذا ابتلعه لأنه يسرط المارة إذا سلكوه كما سمّى لقما لأنه يلتقم السابلة وبالصاد من قلب السين صادا لأجل الطاء وهي اللغة الفصيحة . والصراط المستقيم هو : الدين الحق الذي لا يقبل اللّه تعالى غيره من العباد وانما سمى الدين صراطا ، لأنه يؤدى من يسلكه إلى الجنة كما أن الصراط يؤدى من يسلكه إلى مقصده . وفي اختيار الصراط على الطريق والسبيل تذكرة للصراط الذي هو جسر ممدود بين طرفي جهنم . سهل اللّه علينا وروده وعبوره .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Concerning the phrase “Guide us to the straight path”: ṣirāṭ, also pronounced sirāṭ with sīn, is a broad road. In the second pronunciation it is associated with saraṭa, “to swallow,” because the road as it were swallows those who travel over it; another path-name similarly evokes taking passers-by into itself. With ṣād, the sīn has been changed to ṣād because of the adjoining emphatic ṭāʾ, and this is the more eloquent form of the word.

The “straight path” is the true religion which God accepts from His servants. It is called a path because the one who follows it is led to Paradise, just as a road carries its walker to a destination. Choosing ṣirāṭ rather than ṭarīq or sabīl also recalls the bridge stretched across Hell, which travellers must pass. May God make coming to it and crossing it easy for us.

### Editorial/source qualification

Attributions/etymologies require lexicographic control; religious metaphor belongs to glossator.

<a id="hwn-034"></a>
## Note 34 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [138](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/138?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله وتارة باللام نحو ان هذا القرآن يهدى للتي هي أقوم : اى الملة التي أو الحالة التي أو الطريقة التي ، وفي الحذف إشارة إلى أن المحذوف من الفخامة مبلغا لا يمكن ذكره . لا يقال : الموصول لكونه اسما لا صفة لا يقتضى ذكر موصوف قبله فلا حذف هناك والإشارة المذكورة انّما هي في الموصول دون الحذف . لأنا نقول : الحذف على قسمين : أحدهما حذف ما لا بد منه في تصحيح اللفظ والاخر ما بد منه في تصحيحه كحذف الفاعل مما يبنى للمفعول مثلا وقوله تعالى : « يَهْدِي لِلَّتِي هِيَ أَقْوَمُ » من قبيل الثاني .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On the construction with lām, exemplified by “This Qurʾān guides to that which is most upright”: the implied noun could be “religious community,” “condition,” or “way”—the one that is most upright. Omitting the noun hints at such grandeur in what is meant that it cannot adequately be expressed.

Do not object that the relative pronoun allatī is itself a noun, not an adjective requiring a head noun to be mentioned beforehand, so that nothing has been suppressed and the alleged implication of grandeur arises only from the pronoun itself. We reply that omission is of two sorts: omission of what is required to render an expression grammatically complete, and omission of what the expression can dispense with while remaining complete, such as the agent suppressed in a passive construction. The Qurʾānic wording belongs to the second class.

### Editorial/source qualification

Syntactic necessity and rhetorical ellipsis clearly differentiated.

<a id="hwn-035"></a>
## Note 35 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [138](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/138?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

بصيغة الافراد على ما هو في أكثر النسخ ، اى : على الاستعمال الثاني وهو استعمالها متعدية بالغير سواء كان ذلك الغير هو « إلى » أو « اللام » . وقد يصحف بصيغة التّثنية ، اى : على استعمالها متعدية باللام واستعمالها متعدية بالى . وعبارة المصنف في الحاشية بصيغة الافراد وقد نقلناه آنفا .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

It is in the singular form according to most copies. That is, it refers to the “second usage” of the verb, namely its transitive construction by means of another word, regardless of whether that word is ilā or lām. It can be misread in the dual form, which would refer to the two constructions separately, one transitive with lām and the other with ilā. The author's wording in his gloss is singular, and we quoted it just above.

### Editorial/source qualification

Reported textual difference from the later glossator, NOT certified manuscript variant.

<a id="hwn-036"></a>
## Note 36 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [138](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/138?lang=ar) · [139](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/139?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb, p.7, quoted. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله سواء الطريق اى وسطه : قال الراغب في المفردات : ومكان سوى وسواء وسط ويقال : سواء وسوى وسوى اى : يستوى طرفاه ويستعمل ذلك وصفا وظرفا وأصل ذلك مصدر وقال في سواء الجحيم وسواء السبيل ، قال : والاستقامة تقال في الطريق الذي يكون على خط مستو وبه شبه طريق المحق نحو « اهدنا الصراط المستقيم » . « وان هذا صراطي مستقيما » . « على صراط مستقيم » . وقول الشارح : اى وسطه الذي يفضى سالكه إلى المطلوب البتة ، ليس معنى لغويا لسواء الطريق ، بل الوسط من حيث هو لا يتعدى معناه اللغوي وليس في معناه اللغوي الافضاء بسالكه إلى المطلوب وانما هو من مقارناته الخارجية فان كل من اخذ وسط الطريق المنتهى إلى غاية ومقصد من المقاصد ولم ينحرف عنه لم يتلبد عليه سنن الطريق فلم ينحرف عنه إلى المتاهات وإذا كان على هذا الوصف وصل إلى المطلوب البتة .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On “the middle of the road,” al-Rāghib says in al-Mufradāt that sawāʾ and suwan describe a central or evenly balanced place: its two sides are equal. The expression can serve as a description or an adverb, and originally is a verbal noun. Qurʾānic examples include “the midst of the Fire” and “the middle of the road.” “Straightness” applies literally to a road running on an even line, from which one compares the road of truth to a straight path—“Guide us to the straight path,” “This is My straight path,” and similar expressions.

The commentator's additional words, “the middle that unfailingly brings its traveller to the sought destination,” are not part of the bare dictionary meaning of sawāʾ al-ṭarīq. The lexical sense stops at “middle.” Reaching the objective is an attendant consequence or association of keeping to the middle of a real road, not contained in the term's dictionary meaning. Someone who maintains a middle course on a road leading to a destination without veering off will not lose the path among the byways; while retaining that course he reaches his goal.

### Editorial/source qualification

From Q138 into Q139. Must not assign this quoted al-Taqrīb explanation to Taftāzānī's matn.

<a id="hwn-037"></a>
## Note 37 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [139](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/139?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm; al-Jawharī cited. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله يفضى سالكه إلى المطلوب : اى يأمنه من تغليظ الغير . ولفظ « البتة » مصدر لا يستعمله العرب الا بالألف واللام فان حذفهما خطاء عندهم . وقد يجيء بلا تعريف ( اى : بدون الألف واللام ) كما انشد اليزيدي في مناظرته مع « الكسائي » : فان من خيرهم وأكرمهم * أو خيرهم بتة أبو بكر . ويفهم من كلام الجوهري ان « ال » غير لازم له . قال الجوهري : ويقال : لا افعله « بتة » ولا افعله « البتة » لكل امر لا رجعة فيه . ونصبه على المصدر وقيل إنه ( اى : ان لفظ ال ) لا يحذف الا في الضرورة . ثم إنهم اختلفوا في ان الصيغة هل هي منصرفة أو غير منصرفة للتأنيث والعلمية فإنه علم لقطع خاص في اى مكان يقع فعدم دخول التنوين على الأول لأجل اللام وعلى الثاني للمنع من الصرف .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On “it leads its traveller to the desired goal”: that is, it keeps him safe from being misdirected by someone else. As for the expression al-batta (“absolutely,” “decisively”), it is a verbal noun which, some say, the Arabs use only with the definite article; omitting al- would on that account be erroneous. Yet it also occurs without the article. Al-Yazīdī recited in his disputation with al-Kisāʾī: “Among them the best and noblest—or simply the best of them, decidedly—is Abū Bakr.”

The wording of al-Jawharī implies that the definite article is not obligatory. He says that one can say “I shall not do it batta” or “I shall not do it al-batta,” both signifying something to which there is no return. The accusative is that of a verbal noun; others say the article can be omitted only by poetic necessity.

There is also disagreement whether the form admits regular declension or is diptotic by virtue of femininity and proper-noun status. On one analysis it names a special kind of severance wherever it occurs. Hence the absence of tanwīn may be due either to the article or to restriction from ordinary declension.

### Editorial/source qualification

P139 lexical note; initial phrase text may require photo review.

<a id="hwn-038"></a>
## Note 38 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [139](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/139?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قال المصنف : « الكناية في اللغة مصدر قولك : كنيت بكذا عن كذا وكنوت إذا تركت التصريح به وهي في الاصطلاح يطلق على معنيين : أحدهما : المعنى المصدري الذي هو فعل المتكلم اعني ذكر اللازم وإرادة الملزوم مع جواز إرادة اللازم أيضا . فاللّفظ مكنى به والمعنى مكنى عنه . والثاني نفس اللفظ » . انتهى واعلم : ان هذا بناء على ظاهر مذهب « السكاكى » وامّا على مذهب غيره فهي ذكر الملزوم وإرادة اللازم مع جواز إرادة الملزوم أيضا وما نحن فيه صالح لكل منهما إذ كل من سواء الطريق والطريق المستوى لازم وملزوم بالنسبة إلى الاخر وإلى هذا المعنى أشار المحشى بقوله : « إذ هما متلازمان » فان التلازم من الطرفين .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The author said: Kināya in its linguistic sense is the verbal noun of “I referred indirectly to this by that,” meaning that I refrained from naming it openly. Technically it has two uses. One denotes the speaker's operation: mentioning what necessarily accompanies a thing while intending the thing accompanied, though it remains possible also to intend the accompanying meaning. The overt wording is what is used as an indirect expression, while the intended meaning is what is alluded to. The second usage denotes the expression itself.

This account follows the apparent position of al-Sakkākī. According to others, the operation is to mention what is accompanied while intending what accompanies it, with the possibility of intending the overt meaning too. The present case suits both explanations: “the middle of the road” and “the straight road” are mutually attendant, each capable of being understood as corresponding to what the other implies. The glossator's remark “for the two entail one another” points exactly to this reciprocity.

### Editorial/source qualification

Rhetorical differentia of lāzim/ ملزوم carefully preserved.

<a id="hwn-039"></a>
## Note 39 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [139](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/139?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله كناية عن الطريق المستوى : وذلك لأنه لو كان المراد بوسط الطريق معناه الظاهري لما كان معنى الكلام متعارفا كما لا يخفى . وقوله : « وهذا مراد من فسره » إشارة إلى دفع ما أورد على « الفاضل الدواني » حيث فسره في حاشيته على تلك الرسالة سواء الطريق بالطريق المستوى والصراط المستقيم فاعترضوا عليه بأنه جعل السواء بمعنى الاستواء ثم استعمله بمعنى المستوى ، فجعل كلام المصنف من قبيل إضافة الصفة إلى الموصوف كجرد قطيفة وهذا تكلف . إذ المتعارف بين أهل العرف هو ان سواء الطريق بمعنى وسطه كما أن سواء الجحيم بمعنى وسطه وأيضا لو كان من باب إضافة الصفة إلى الموصوف كان المقصود اظهار الحمد على ايصاله إلى الطريق المستقيم وإذا كان بمعنى الوسط فيكون المقصود : الحمد على ايصاله إلى وسط الطريق وهذا أقوى من الأول فالحمد عليه أولى . وحاصل الدفع ان ليس غرضه من ذلك التفسير ان معنى السواء ذلك ، بل غرضه بيان حاصل المعنى المقصود من وسط الطريق كناية فلذا فسره بالطريق المستوى والصراط المستقيم .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On the statement that this is an indirect expression for the level or straight road: if “middle of the road” were meant in its unqualified literal sense, the sentence would not express the familiar intended meaning.

The comment “That is what the one who interpreted it intended” answers an objection to al-Fāḍil al-Dawwānī. Dawwānī had interpreted sawāʾ al-ṭarīq as “the straight road” and “the upright path.” Critics objected that he first made sawāʾ mean straightness and then used it as though it meant “straight,” turning the author's wording into an awkward genitive of a quality with its bearer, like a thick cloak. This seems strained because ordinary speakers understand sawāʾ al-ṭarīq as “its middle,” just as they understand sawāʾ al-jaḥīm as “the middle of Hell.”

They also argued that taking it as a straight road would make the purpose simply to praise God for bringing one to the straight road; taking it as the middle would make the purpose to praise Him for bringing one to the middle of that road, a stronger favour more deserving of thanks. The answer is that Dawwānī did not intend to redefine the literal lexical meaning of sawāʾ. He wished to explain the meaning ultimately intended *through the figurative expression* “the middle of the road,” and therefore paraphrased its intended referent as the upright path.

### Editorial/source qualification

The debate is about whether Dawwānī was redefining a word or interpreting a kināya.

<a id="hwn-040"></a>
## Note 40 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [139](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/139?lang=ar) · [140](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/140?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

يعنى : المراد بسواء الطريق اما نفس الامر عموما اى : حقيقة الامر بعمومه سواء كان ملة الإسلام أم غيره ليشمل علمي الكلام والمنطق اللذين يبحث في هذا الكتاب عن مسائلهما أو خصوص ملة الإسلام فيكون تلميحا على قوله تعالى : « اهْدِنَا الصِّراطَ الْمُسْتَقِيمَ » .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The “middle of the road” may mean reality itself in general—that is, the truth of matters broadly, whether the particular issue belongs to Islam or to another field. That reading covers both theological doctrine and logic, the two sciences whose problems this book treats. Alternatively it may mean specifically the religious community or dispensation of Islam, in which case the words hint at the Qurʾānic request “Guide us to the straight path” (1:6).

### Editorial/source qualification

Q139 ends mid-note; Q140 completes its choice between universal truth and Islam-specific image.
