<a id="hwn-085"></a>
## Note 85 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [149](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/149?lang=ar) · [150](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/150?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī (following a report attributed to ʿAbd al-Raḥīm). **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله أو مستقر خبر لمبتدأ

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The discussion begins with a question about why the second clause alone receives the parsing “a stable adverbial serving as a predicate of a subject.” The first clause could also be construed that way, and the difference in grammatical treatment needs an explanation.

The ordinary rule is that a laghw adverbial is related to a governor explicitly stated, while a mustaqarr adverbial has an omitted governor. The former can be called “idle” because it exercises no independent syntactic operation, neither overtly nor in an implied pronoun; or because its governor's pronoun has not “settled” within it. In a sentence such as “Zayd is in the house,” the verb understood as its governor is absent, and its pronominal relation is said to have settled in the prepositional phrase.

Another tradition distinguishes general existential verbs from verbs specifying a particular activity: the predicative adverbial would have a general governor (being, existing, obtaining), while the ordinary adjunct would depend on a specific action, whether that action is actually expressed or omitted. This differs from simply classifying by the presence or absence of the governor.

A passage attributed to al-Sayyid al-Sharīf in his marginal notes on al-Kashshāf complicates the matter. An omitted governor need not always be a general existential verb. If one says “Zayd on the horse,” “Zayd among the scholars” or “Zayd in Basra,” the intended activities may be riding, being counted among scholars, or living in Basra. The general verb is sufficient for a formal grammatical analysis, but a particular verb may explain the sentence more exactly. This suggests that a predicative phrase can remain predicative even when the semantic governor is particular.

The glossator objects that the distinction between approaches is not merely verbal. Some expressions require a specific supplied action, such as “who will guarantee the refined text for you?” A bare “being” cannot reproduce their meaning. It is possible to reconcile the rival claims if “general existential governor” describes the most usual case rather than an exclusive rule.

In four syntactic positions—predicate, relative clause, descriptive adjective and circumstance—grammarians commonly require an understood general governor to be suppressed, since the context already indicates it and the adverbial takes its place. Ibn Jinnī reportedly permits its overt expression, but al-Raḍī rejects the alleged supporting evidence. The verse containing “settled with him” can be interpreted as resting motionless rather than simply existing. Where an expressed existential word does occur in a revered sermon or verse, it may denote a particular form of indwelling or rest rather than unrestricted being. Other poetic exceptions are treated as necessity or similarly particular meanings.

Outside those four positions, a prepositional expression is ordinarily related to an explicit governor, according to al-Raḍī. If the intended activity is specific, it may be omitted when an indicator permits recovery: “I prayed in the mosque and Zayd [prayed] in the bathhouse—which of our prayers is preferable?” Finally, some scholars prefer to rename laghw “particular” and mustaqarr “general,” to avoid calling Qurʾānic or prophetic wording “idle.” Al-Sakkākī's preference for calling the terminal rhythm of Qurʾānic verses “verse-endings” rather than “rhymed prose” offers a parallel concern for decorum.

### Editorial/source qualification

Extended two-page first-pass analytic rendering of the full named discussion; individual Arabic quotations, poetical details and the exact initial speaker seam require verification.

<a id="hwn-086"></a>
## Note 86 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [151](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/151?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī, reporting al-Raḍī and other grammarians. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

الغايات هي الظروف التي قطعت عن الإضافة وبنيت

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The “termini” (ghāyāt) are adverbials which originally stood in a genitive construction but have been detached from their complement and become indeclinable. Why call these expressions termini? The thing named as an adverbial normally indicates a *relation* to another term rather than itself being the endpoint. When the complement—the true limiting point—is omitted, its signification is incorporated into the remaining adverbial, which is then called a terminus by extension. Al-Raḍī is cited for this explanation. The words kull and baʿḍ, although also sometimes detached from a complement, are not ordinarily classed as ghāyāt, since their omission is compensated in a different way.

Another explanation says that these expressions may follow an explicit mention of the individual to which they are related and therefore take the name “termini.” A third suggestion extends the name to all six spatial directions, since each implies a boundary or endpoint. The transmitted inventory of the special indeclinable adverbials includes “before,” “after,” “below,” “above,” “in front,” “ahead,” “behind,” “to the rear,” “lower,” “short of,” “first,” and special forms of “on high.” Their grammatically privileged treatment is based on attested use; it should not be extended by analogy to every synonym such as “right,” “left,” or “last.”

### Editorial/source qualification

The quoted al-Taqrīb note immediately afterward is separate. List is traditional descriptive inventory, not a logically necessary closure under synonymy.

### Separately attributed UNNUMBERED gloss — not a numbered note

**Source voice:** Al-Taqrīb, p.10, separately introduced after note 86; NOT assigned an additional HWN number. Working render, no independent photo verification.

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قولـه وبعد هو من الغايات

</div>

Al-Taqrīb explains that in “I came after Zayd,” the coming of Zayd marks the boundary before my coming; in “I came before Zayd,” his coming marks the boundary after mine. When the genitive complement signifying that limiting point is omitted, “before,” “after,” and related adverbs stand in its place and acquire the designation “terminus.”

The words refer to “after” (baʿd) itself, not to the class of “termini.” In strict usage the latter term applies only to adverbials which have lost their expressed complement and have become indeclinable; they therefore possess only the single grammatical state under discussion. Unless one uses “termini” figuratively for the adverbials in *all* their states, or adopts the broad use attributed above to some grammarians, one should not attribute the rule indiscriminately to the whole class.

## Note 88 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [151](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/151?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله أما أن يكون نسيا منسيا

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The word nasy, read with kasra or fatḥa on the first radical, evokes the Qurʾānic expression “a thing forgotten and abandoned” (19:23). One explanation takes it as an insignificant article abandoned at a traveller's former stopping-place; another as something of a kind naturally discarded, such as a cloth no longer used. In the grammatical classification at issue, a complement may therefore be genuinely left out of consideration, rather than silently held in mind.

The opposing description, “intended in the mind” (manwiyyan), means a suppressed term which the speaker continues to regard mentally even though it is absent from the utterance. These two types of omission must not be conflated.

### Editorial/source qualification

Lexical meanings are reported glosses; exact morphological etymology to be checked with lexicon.

## Note 89 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [151](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/151?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله وعلى الثالث مبنية

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

In the third case an adverbial is indeclinable because its genitive complement is not pronounced yet remains intended. The adverbial consequently resembles a particle in its need for something outside its overt form. That resemblance motivates indeclinability.

The second case differs: the missing complement has been altogether forgotten, and the utterance no longer depends on it to express the intended thought. Expressions equivalent to “I was beforehand” or “I was afterward” then convey simply “I was formerly” or “I was later,” without comparison to some separately specified point. The first case also involves dependence on a complement, but there it is actually expressed as an ordinary genitive construction; this visible nominal relation outweighs the resemblance to a particle and preserves declension.

### Editorial/source qualification

Clear three-way contrast: overt complement, absent and not intended, absent but intended.

## Note 90 — PARTIAL / unverified text

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [151](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/151?lang=ar) · [152](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/152?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī with a following separately attributed al-Taqrīb discussion. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله فهذا

</div>

### English — PARTIAL, not complete

The prefixed fāʾ in “and this” may have been supplied because the speaker or copyist regarded an introductory ammā as already understood, since the combination “ammā ... fa-” conventionally requires a response introduced by fāʾ. The expression can therefore be analysed as if it read “as for what follows—this [is ...].”

But what does the demonstrative “this” designate? In the opening of a book, the author may have not yet uttered or inscribed the subsequent text, and no external physical object stands ready for pointing. The relevant matters are present instead in the writer's mind, in an ordered form ready for presentation: meanings to be clothed in words, or words arranged to signify those meanings. A demonstrative can point metaphorically to the mentally present as if it were perceptually before the hearer.

A further argument arises if the preface is composed after the body of the work. Then the demonstrative might seem to refer to the existing marks on the written pages. A quotation from al-Taqrīb objects that a written mark is not simply nothing: written signs are another external representation of expressions, which may be realised in speech or inscription. The exact scope of that argument and the relation to the main note require independent inspection of the numbered-note boundary.

### Editorial/source qualification

Long p151→152 passage intersects a separately quoted al-Taqrīb contribution. Abridged first-pass rendering; not yet a complete segmented translation of note90.

### Separately attributed UNNUMBERED gloss — not a numbered note

**Source voice:** al-Taqrīb p.11, following note90; attribution and seam not independently photographed; NOT assigned an additional HWN number. Working render, no independent photo verification.

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

فهذا هو على تقدير أما بعد فهذا

</div>

The formula can be understood as “Now, after this: this [book].” What is pointed to may be an ordered mental composition, either its meanings already conceived or the expressions intended to convey them. If the preface is written after the work is finished, one can also speak of the already drawn characters. Those characters are outward signs of expressions; the claim that ordered expressions and meanings have no external existence must therefore be handled carefully. This distinction is material to the question whether a book is constituted by intelligible contents, linguistic expressions or physical written signs.

<a id="hwn-091"></a>
## Note 91 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [152](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/152?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله وهذا إشارة إلى المرتب الحاضر في الذهن

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Pointing with “this” to something ordered and present in the mind is a metaphor. In ordinary literal usage one points to what can be seen or sensed in one's presence; things nonexistent, immaterial, or physically remote cannot be pointed to in that way. Their presence to the intellect is treated as if it were sensible presence.

Why did the principal commentary allow just two possible referents—meanings and expressions—when “book” can also signify written marks or combinations of meanings, expressions and marks, yielding seven possibilities? Because the following predicate, “the utmost refinement of speech,” must apply to the thing indicated. Speech can be taken as spoken/verbal expression or as intelligible/mental meaning. It cannot literally be predicated of mere graphic marks, which are not themselves speech, nor of a compound consisting of speech together with another thing. Even combining meanings and words as distinct components creates a difficulty: one would have to use the ambiguous word “speech” in two senses at once or accept an unnecessary figure.

### Editorial/source qualification

The seven referent combinations are enumerated editorially; no complete independent photographed column comparison.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله من المعاني المخصوصة

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The demonstrative might in isolation refer to any of seven candidates: the particular meanings, particular linguistic expressions, graphic marks, each of the three pairwise combinations, or the combination of all three. But only the first two candidates survive in context. The title-like expression “the utmost refinement of speech” is predicated of what “this” denotes, and speech is divided here into its verbal and intelligible senses.

Graphic marks are not literally speech. A compound of marks and either words or meanings is not identical with speech, since a composite differs from each of its constituent parts. Even a composite of verbal expressions and meanings cannot be literally identified with “speech” without using the same equivocal term in two senses simultaneously, a move rejected in this commentator's theory; nor is there reason to impose metaphor when a straightforward referent is available. The two retained options therefore represent the intelligible subject of the subsequent predication, not an arbitrary count of physical media.

### Editorial/source qualification

This closely parallels note91 under a distinct named voice. Preserve as a separate argument.

## Note 93 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [152](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/152?lang=ar) · [153](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/153?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله سواء

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The word sawāʾ is a verbal-noun-like expression meaning equality or equivalence and can be employed adjectivally, as in the Qurʾānic invitation to a word equal between the parties. The following construction is treated as the subject of the sentence and sawāʾ as its predicate: “its occurrence before composition or after composition is equal.”

An objection argues that aw (“or”) presents one member of several alternatives, while equality requires a relation between *both* members. Should the sentence not use wa (“and”) instead? The answer is that aw can, in familiar usage, serve the function of wa in such contexts. A stricter objection arises if one insists on the paired interrogative construction with am, as in “It is the same whether you warn them or do not warn them.” The detailed technical comparison is referred to discussions in the marginalia on al-Muṭawwal.

### Editorial/source qualification

Printed note crosses Q152/153; the web text stops at 'أو' and continues on the next page.

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [153](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/153?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

الغرض من هذا الكلام رد ما ذكره بعض الأعلام

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The purpose of the preceding remark is to reject a distinction made by certain scholars: if the preface was written before composing the book, “this” supposedly refers to meanings arranged mentally; if written afterward, it supposedly refers to things existing externally. The basis of the response can be understood from the earlier analysis of what a demonstrative indicates. The glossator nevertheless suggests that the rejection is not entirely beyond scrutiny, and asks the reader to reflect.

### Editorial/source qualification

Explicit hesitation in the source; not an unequivocal endorsement of either branch.

<a id="hwn-095"></a>
## Note 95 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [153](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/153?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله أو بعده

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Many writers use aw after sawāʾ (“it is equal whether ... or ...”), but a number of grammarians prohibit this and insist on am. Abū ʿAlī argues that aw signifies just one of the alternatives: “Whether I stand or sit, it is equal to me” would then assert equality concerning an unspecified *one*, whereas equality must concern *both*. Ibn Hishām follows him and describes al-Jawharī's example using aw as an inadvertence; he also treats a Qurʾānic reading using aw in that construction as unusual.

The glossator contends that the prohibition depends on a particular parsing. If sawāʾ is an advanced predicate and the two alternatives constitute a later subject, the supposed difficulty may appear. But al-Raḍī offers another analysis: sawāʾ is the predicate of an understood subject, such as “the two matters are equal to me.” The following interrogative clauses explicate the two matters and can be construed conditionally: “If I stand or if I sit, the two states are equal to me.” On that analysis both am and aw are possible, because the equal status of the two situations was already established by the suppressed nominal construction.

The objection can be turned against the restrictive analysis itself: am, no less than aw, can identify one among alternatives. If that alone made the sentence defective, the version with am would also fail. What matters is the syntactic explanation of the expression as a whole, not merely the lexical signification of its connective. The writer regards the second explanation as the more satisfactory.

### Editorial/source qualification

Explicit disagreement between Abū ʿAlī, Ibn Hishām and al-Raḍī is the substance of the passage.

<a id="hwn-096"></a>
## Note 96 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [153](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/153?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Jalāl al-Dīn al-Dawwānī (source signature). **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله إذ لا وجود للألفاظ ولا للمعاني في الخارج

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The statement that neither ordered words nor their meanings exist externally calls into question an earlier proposal: if a preface is composed after the treatise has been written, its “this” could point to the now-existing book rather than merely to contents in the mind. What exists externally in that case is a particular collection of *graphic marks*, not the universal words or intelligible meanings as such. Yet it would be awkward to call those marks, considered just as marks, “the utmost refinement of speech.” One could treat the marks metonymically, naming the signifying object by the thing signified; but that still requires qualification.

The present demonstrative is not meant to designate merely the particular unique written copy while excluding another copy that conveys the same linguistic and intellectual content. It denotes a type of ordered discourse, whether found in this physical copy or another sharing its significant structure. Such a universal type is not present externally as a single immediately observable thing. Accordingly, even when an actual written copy lies before the author, the referent of the title and demonstrative is primarily mentally conceived. This provides a reason for treating titles of books as names of kinds rather than merely the proper names of individual physical codices.

### Editorial/source qualification

Source is attributed to Dawwānī; argument distinguishes token document vs kind rather than asserting that no spoken or written marks exist externally.

<a id="hwn-097"></a>
## Note 97 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [153](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/153?lang=ar) · [154](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/154?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

الكلام اللفظي ما يتلفظ به الإنسان والكلام النفسي

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Verbal speech (kalām lafẓī) is what a person actually articulates. Mental speech (kalām nafsī) is what a person conceives inwardly and in accordance with which spoken words may then be produced. For example, “Zayd is standing” may be considered as outwardly uttered words, while the mental representation or judgment corresponding to them is called mental speech. The source also describes the mind's organized counterpart of the uttered statement as corresponding to that external expression.

The names “verbal speech” and “mental speech” are intelligible with reference to these two sides. The words do not, merely by themselves, settle the deeper theological debate over whether mental speech is a distinct eternal divine attribute or a created psychological act; that would require the respective kalām context rather than assuming the same technical use throughout.

### Editorial/source qualification

Note crosses Q153–154. Short working rendition; the published searchable text's precise assertion of correspondence and syntax requires photo check.

قوله حمله على هذا

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The purpose of the proposed parsing is to rescue the author's apparently problematic predication. In literal terms an abstract meaning such as “refinement” cannot be predicated directly of a concrete object such as a particular book, just as was explained previously in connection with “He sent him as guidance.” Here the principal commentator proposes either emphatic figurative predication or the supply of an omitted predicate. He does not treat the abstract noun as a passive participle, although he had earlier allowed a verbal noun to stand for an active participle: the genitive combination with “speech” makes such an analysis less straightforward. One might still argue for an adjective–substantive construction, but that is not the chosen account. Other possible resolutions of abstract-to-concrete predication were already described earlier and remain conceptually available.

The authorial hierarchy is T matn → Y commentary → H gloss; this is an H speaker reconstructing Y's repair of T wording.

<a id="hwn-099"></a>
## Note 99 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [154](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/154?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله أما على المبالغة

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

There is an apparent mismatch in declaring “this book is the utmost refinement of speech”: the book is a concrete item whereas “refinement” (tahdhīb) is an action or abstract quality, and an abstract cannot literally be predicated of a concrete individual. The commentator's first solution is rhetorical intensification: the book is identified with refinement itself by an emphatic metaphor, as in “Zayd is justice.” On one explanation the abstract predicate bears no independent reference to the subject, though the Kufan grammatical tradition may instead analyse a verbal noun as derived and therefore capable of carrying a pronominal relation while receiving a more literal predication.

A second solution supplies an adjective or implied predicate: “this book is refined.” There are several possible reconstructions of the original emphatic verbal expression. The governing verb of an absolute object (mafʿūl muṭlaq) may have been omitted, the cognate accusative placed in its position, and a modifier then substituted in its turn. One could understand, for example, “this book was refined with the utmost refinement,” with successive ellipses. Another possibility inserts an omitted genitive: “the composition of this book is the utmost refinement of discourse.” A further approach understands “refinement” as “refined discourse,” treating the verbal noun as passive in sense and making the genitive relation explanatory.

An objection closes the discussion. If refinement means removing defects and superfluous material, how can this particular book be described as pure refinement when, as the glossator admits, it contains certain matters that might be judged surplus? The issue will recur later. This objection is not by itself a complete independent textual proof that the author wrote any particular surplus clause.

### Editorial/source qualification

The substantive alternatives are rendered; some intermediate nominal ellipses need line-by-line Arabic pairing during later manuscript collation.

<a id="hwn-102"></a>
## Note 102 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [154](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/154?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله على طريقة مجاز الحذف

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The author explains in his commentary on the Talkhīṣ that a word may be called figurative not only because its ordinary meaning is transferred, but also because the grammatical construction of the sentence changes through an omitted or added expression. As examples of omission he cites Qurʾānic wording conventionally understood as “the command of your Lord came” or “ask the people of the town.” In the latter case one asks the inhabitants, not the buildings—though God's power to make buildings speak is not denied. As an example of an additional expression he cites “There is nothing like unto His likeness,” whose intended denial is that nothing is like God, not merely that nothing resembles a second likeness distinct from Him.

The construction under discussion belongs to the first category: a phrase has been left unspoken. The reason for positing it is that without a supplied expression the surface predication would be unsuitable. This is an interpretive argument from grammatical coherence, not independent evidence of a physically omitted manuscript word.

### Editorial/source qualification

Examples concern the historical theory of majāz al-ḥadhf; precise theological implications must remain source-attributed.

<a id="hwn-103"></a>
## Note 103 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [154](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/154?lang=ar) · [155](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/155?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī, reporting al-Dawwānī on the ending. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

فإن التحرير هو البيان الخالي عن الحشو والزوائد

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Taḥrīr is clarification or explanation freed from unnecessary padding and surplus expressions. Bayān is broader: it can designate explanation whether or not it meets that standard. Taḥrīr can also refer specifically to setting out an intended meaning in writing, just as taqrīr can refer to presenting it in articulate exposition. This lexical distinction was perhaps not pursued by the principal commentator because it adds relatively little to the present reasoning.

The following preposition “in” expresses a figurative containment. Broad conceptual inclusion is likened to the spatial inclusion of something in a vessel, and the particle ordinarily signifying physical location is transferred to the conceptual relation. Such a figurative container cannot be assumed to reveal a new literal physical object. A source note ascribed to al-Dawwānī supplies a formulation of the first explanation at the beginning of the next printed page.

### Editorial/source qualification

First-pass across Q154→155; precise last-clause attribution and what 'الأول' resumes need photograph-assisted check.

<a id="hwn-104"></a>
## Note 104 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [155](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/155?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله والمنطق آلة قانونية

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On “logic is a rule-governed instrument”: an instrument is an intermediary between an agent and the thing affected by the agent, through which the agent's effect reaches its recipient—like the pen between the writer and what is written. The word qānūn is said to come from Greek or Syriac, originally used for a ruling line in writing. In technical usage a canon is a universal proposition from which rulings concerning its subject's particular cases are learned. The term will be discussed in greater detail later. Thinking is the arrangement of known matters to obtain matters not yet known.

Logic is called an *instrument* because it mediates between the rational power and the theoretical objects it seeks to acquire. It is called *rule-governed* because its problems formulate universal rules by which judgments concerning particular instances become known. For example, if one knows the rule that a universal affirmative converts to a particular affirmative, one can infer from “Every human is an animal” that “Some animal is human.” This example presupposes the intended existential context; it does not automatically settle the modern empty-domain interpretation of a universal proposition.

The definition ascribes protection from error to the *observance* of logical rules. Possessing the discipline itself does not confer infallibility: otherwise anyone trained in logic would never err, whereas one can plainly make mistakes by neglecting the art and failing to apply its precepts.

The definition's elements can now be distinguished. “Instrument” functions like a genus; “rule-governed” like a more remote differentia, excluding the particular tools used by craftsmen; and its final qualification like a nearer differentia, distinguishing logic from other rule-governed studies that preserve correctness in speech rather than in thinking—for instance grammar and morphology.

Strictly, however, the definition is a *rasm* (descriptive definition), not a *ḥadd* (essential definition). An essential definition uses intrinsic constituents. Logic's instrumentality is relational to other disciplines, not something constituting its essence considered in isolation. Error-prevention is also its end, which is extrinsic to the thing defined; defining a science through its purpose is therefore descriptive.

An objection is that logic can be instrumental in relation to logic itself: one logical proposition is used to establish another. The initial answer says that such use occurs between distinct parts, rather than the entire science being instrumental to itself; to make instrumentality essential to the whole, one would need each problem to be instrumental to itself. The glossator suggests that the reply is more elaborate than necessary. Even if logic has some reflexive uses, its instrumental relation to another discipline is enough to show that the relationship is not intrinsic to logic as such. An essential attribute must belong to a thing through what it is, not merely through comparison with something else.

### Editorial/source qualification

Working source-based full first-pass of note104 on instrumentality, qānūn, observance, rasm/ḥadd, reflective use. Arabic locator only; original printed page and internal quotations require photo collation.

<a id="hwn-106"></a>
## Note 106 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [155](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/155?lang=ar) · [156](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/156?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله على نهج قانون الإسلام

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On “according to the rule or method of Islam”: this qualification distinguishes kalām from philosophical inquiry, since philosophers also investigate the conditions of the first principle and the return, yet do not formally require their conclusions to conform to the religious law of Islam as a condition of the inquiry.

The intended exclusion should not be exaggerated. The point is that philosophical investigation does not *make conformity to the revealed law a formal criterion*, not that it positively requires its findings to *contradict* that law. Were one to assume the latter, a philosopher who accepts philosophy would, simply by that fact, be obliged to adopt positions contrary to the purified religious law and thus be judged an unbeliever; that does not follow from the methodological distinction. **Not taking a property into account is logically different from taking its absence into account.** The negative qualification on the criterion is not a positive criterion of nonconformity.

### Editorial/source qualification

Important contrast between the absence of a formal constraint and a positive contradictory constraint; not a global judgment on philosophers' religious status.

<a id="hwn-107"></a>
## Note 107 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [156](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/156?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

الظاهر من كلامه أن العامل في المعطوف

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The apparent assumption is that the governor of the coordinated expression is the same governor that acts on the first expression, operating through the coordinating particle; this is the majority position and is regarded here as sound. Others assign the grammatical operation directly to the coordinating particle, while yet others posit an implicit governor after it. A further view describes the coordinated word as governed by the word with which it is coordinated; arguments can be found for this account as well.

One might instead coordinate the disputed expression with “refinement” (taḥrīr), as reported from al-Dawwānī. The whole would then mean that the work represents the utmost refinement of discourse in bringing the intended objectives nearer—that is, arranging an argument so that the desired conclusion follows. The glossator finds that construction closer to the superficial wording but less fitting to the intended sense, and suggests that this is why the principal commentator does not examine it.

### Editorial/source qualification

Speaker signatures and exactly which coordination is under discussion need the printed column or immediately preceding principal lemma.

<a id="hwn-110"></a>
## Note 110 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [156](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/156?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

يعني أن كلمة من لبيان الجنس

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The preposition min is understood as explicating a genus or class (bayān al-jins). It occurs especially often after the indefinite expressions mā and mahmā because of their breadth and ambiguity, as in Qurʾānic constructions equivalent to “whatever verse We abrogate” and “whatever sign you bring us.” Yet it can also follow other words, as in the Qurʾānic prohibition concerning the abomination of idols.

The prepositional phrase functions as a circumstantial adjunct (ḥāl) and is therefore in the accusative position in syntactic analysis. One can reconstruct it with an understood “being from the establishment of the doctrines of Islam.” The same applies to analogous constructions.

Certain grammarians reject the category of explanatory min altogether. On their approach, this particular min is construed partitively instead. The difference between a genus-explaining and a partitive reading remains an interpretive alternative rather than one settled by the mere appearance of the preposition.

### Editorial/source qualification

Two grammatical schools' analyses retained; Qurʾānic examples are text-specific and need verse-by-verse cross-check.

---

<a id="hwn-111"></a>
## Note 111 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [156](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/156?lang=ar) · [157](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/157?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله والإضافة في عقائد الإسلام بيانية

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On the assertion that the genitive construction in “the doctrines of Islam” (ʿaqāʾid al-Islām) is explanatory: the conventional account of semantic genitive constructions usually reduces them to the meanings of lām (belonging, specification) or, less commonly, fī (containment in a time or place). To determine which applies, one first considers how the extensions of the two terms are related. The standard four logical relations are equality, complete separation, inclusion of one under the other, and overlap without complete inclusion.

If the two terms are coextensive, the genitive relation is normally inadmissible unless the terms are interpreted in a way that changes their relation into one of the other three classes. If they are wholly separate, one must ask whether a meaningful relation exists at all. Without one, a genitive construction cannot be justified. With one, the specific relation governs its interpretation. If the second term denotes the time or place containing the first, as in “the stratagem of night,” “the waiting of four months,” or “the inhabitants of the prison,” the construction can mean “in.” Otherwise it is ordinarily understood in terms of lām, covering numerous relations: a place designated for an action, a person's limb or speech, something owned, something associated with a person, and kinship.

In a relation of complete inclusion, the construction may be impossible as ordinarily stated if the second term is broader and the first denotes an instance not properly expressible by that genitive form; but if a suitable reinterpretation can be made, the familiar lām construction returns. Conversely, a first term and a more restricted second can be combined as “Sunday's day,” “the science of jurisprudence,” or “the arāk tree,” according to their conceptual functions. Where the terms overlap, a genitive meaning “from” may arise if the second designates the material or origin of the first; otherwise lām remains available.

Apply this to “beliefs of Islam.” If “Islam” is taken as the beliefs themselves, their conceptual relation to the doctrines requires careful analysis. The glossator argues that, under the stated general rules, lām would be the expected interpretation. He understands the principal commentator's proposed explanatory construction as relying on another scholar's judgment that the semantic sense is clearer if an implicit “from” is made explicit. But he objects that this conflicts with the grammatical distribution just rehearsed, calling it a departure from the received agreement.

The discussion is thus not merely about theological ownership. It tests a genitive phrase against a taxonomy of relations between extensions, moving from general logic of universals to a choice among grammatical readings. The accusation of consensus violation belongs to this particular glossator, not to a newly established historical consensus.

### Editorial/source qualification

Long cross-page first-pass translation of Q156–157. Several of the author's intermediate examples are interpreted conceptually; the exact printed taxonomic divisions should be photographically checked.

<a id="hwn-112"></a>
## Note 112 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [157](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/157?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm; following passage by Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī separate. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله وإن كان عبارة عن مجموع الإقرار باللسان

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

If “Islam” is understood as the composite of verbal profession and its associated acknowledgment, the discussion touches the relationship between Islam and faith (īmān). One report from al-Riḍā is cited as support for treating their meanings as equivalent. By contrast, a view reported from the Shaykh of the school makes faith more comprehensive: its essence is the heart's assent while verbal profession is taken into account in its manifestation.

The glossator supports the inward conception by lexical usage. Īmān in ordinary Arabic signifies accepting or believing someone's statement. The Qurʾānic speech of Joseph's brothers—“You would not believe us”—uses the word in the sense of accepting testimony; another Qurʾānic example speaks of believing in al-jibt and al-ṭāghūt. In colloquial speech too, people say that someone believes or does not believe a particular account. There is no warrant for presuming that religious usage has transferred the word to an altogether different primary signification unless evidence for that transfer is produced.

An objection says that one cannot conventionally recognize inward assent without speech, so outward articulation must be part of the concept of faith. The reply is that an inward conviction may indeed exist in someone unable to speak, or someone who remains silent. The conclusion drawn is that the reality of faith is inward assent, while speech and bodily action are necessary for its appearance and social recognition.

### Editorial/source qualification

Note112 ends at signature (ʿAbd al-Raḥīm). A much longer quotation explicitly introduced as 'Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī said in some marginalia' follows and continues to Q158. It is stored separately, not counted as note113.

### Separately attributed UNNUMBERED gloss — not a numbered note

**Source voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī in separate H gloss quoted after HWN-112, Q157–158; NOT assigned an additional HWN number. Working render, no independent photo verification.

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

اعلم أن الإسلام على ما يستفاد من الأخبار هو الإقرار باللسان

</div>

A separately quoted writer argues that Islam in its broad legal sense consists in professing that there is no god but God and that Muḥammad is His messenger, whether or not inward assent and corresponding action accompany the profession. Islam is therefore broader in extension than faith. He adduces a reported dialogue with Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq distinguishing the outward Muslim status—on which life is protected and the rules of marriage and inheritance operate—from faith as a state established inwardly and shown through righteous action. On that account faith entails Islam in outward practice, whereas outward Islam does not entail inward faith. Qurʾān 49:14, which tells certain desert Arabs to say “We have submitted” rather than “We have believed,” is cited as support. The writer then distinguishes bare verbal acknowledgment from the fuller sense of Islam in which it is accompanied by heartfelt belief and action. This is the cited Imāmī account, including its own assessment of other opinions; no independent authentication of the reported narration has been performed.

<a id="hwn-113"></a>
## Note 113 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [158](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/158?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Printed siglum M–B, unidentified. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

بفتح الجيم القلب الذي هو المراد ههنا

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

With fatḥa on the jīm, janān means the heart; it is given that designation because the heart is concealed within the breast. Another suggested explanation derives the name from the heart's gathering or retaining impressions, though the glossator regards this as remote. The word belongs to the root j-n-n, which conveys covering, hiding or being concealed. Qurʾānic usage describes unborn children (ajinna) within their mothers, similarly hidden from view.

The same root yields jinn, beings described in the inherited lexicon as hidden from ordinary vision; a traditional definition says they may assume different forms. The author distinguishes this from a theological demonstration of the existence or character of these beings. The root also supplies forms denoting darkness or being covered, including the Qurʾānic expression about night covering Abraham.

The garden, janna, takes its name from trees and foliage which shelter and cover the ground; hence the plural jannāt and scriptural descriptions of gardens underneath which rivers flow. With a different vocalisation, junna means a covering or shield: related forms describe a protection against weapons. The lexical collection unites these senses through concealment but does not make them synonymous in every context.

### Editorial/source qualification

Multiple homographic j-n-n words require exact vowelling. Manuscript signature M–B is not independently identified; metaphysical definition of jinn is lexicographic report, not scientifically established.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله فالإضافة لامية : لمكان التباين بين المضاف والمضاف اليه اما على الثاني فظاهر واما على الأول فلضرورة ان المركب يباين الاجزاء كما ترى في السكنجبين بالنسبة إلى الخل والعسل فما توهم من أن العقائد أعم منه مطلقا بهذا المعنى فليس مما يلتفت اليه .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The genitive construction is to be interpreted as involving lām because the first and second terms are distinct in extension. This is obvious under the second analysis. Under the first it follows from the fact that a composite differs from its constituent parts: the medicinal preparation sakanjabīn is not identical with the vinegar and honey of which it is composed. Hence the suggestion that, on this reading, “beliefs” would simply be universally broader than “Islam” is not to be accepted.

<a id="hwn-115"></a>
## Note 115 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [158](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/158?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Full digital searchable-text working transcription. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic working transcription

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله ويحتمل التجوز في الاسناد : بان يجعل الحمد على سبيل المبالغة كما مر مرارا . والتجوز في الاسناد هو اسناد الشئ إلى غير ما هو له مثل « صام نهاره » و « جرى النهر » و « سال الميزاب » وهنا اسناد التبصرة إلى الضمير من هذا القبيل فان الأصل فيه ان يسند إلى فعل الكتاب وشأنه بل فعل المصنّف أو شأنه ، هذا . ويحتمل ان يكون على تقدير المضاف اما قبل التبصرة اى جعلته ذا تبصرة أو قبل الضمير ، اى : جعلت شأنه وحاله تبصرة ، وقس على هذا قوله تذكرة .

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Figurative attribution (majāz fī al-isnād) occurs when an action or description is predicated of something other than its proper bearer, as in “his day fasted,” “the river ran” or “the waterspout flowed.” Here the book is said to provide “insight” (tabṣira), even though strictly it is the action or intellectual work of the book's author—and, derivatively, the book's function—that gives insight.

One can understand the phrase as an intensified predication, or assume an omitted noun: “I made it a possessor of insight” or “I made its condition and function a source of insight.” The same kind of reconstruction applies to the subsequent statement that the book is “a reminder” (tadhkira). The examples clarify a distinction between a property in the subject literally and an effect or instrument attributed to it by association.

### Editorial/source qualification

Full digitally searchable Arabic working transcription from p158, unverified against original photograph.

<a id="hwn-116"></a>
## Note 116 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [158](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/158?lang=ar) · [159](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/159?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

يعني أن فاعل الإفهام ومفعوله كلاهما محذوف

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Both the agent and object of the action of causing understanding (ifhām) have been omitted. Two reconstructions are possible: the teacher or another person could be the agent and the person described by the relative expression its recipient, or conversely the person so described could be the one who imparts understanding and someone else its recipient.

On the second interpretation, there is a straightforward parallel for the definite article standing in place of a suppressed third-person pronoun, as in Qurʾānic or other established constructions. The first interpretation raises a more technical objection: replacement by the definite article is commonly explained when the thing suppressed is pronominal, whereas here it might replace a noun that would otherwise be overt.

The reply is twofold. First, the case need not be analysed as suppression followed by substitution: a noun or pronoun can simply be suppressed on the strength of a contextual indication, and that licence is more general. Second, even granting that it is a case of replacement, there is no proof that substitution by the definite article is restricted to absent third-person pronouns; it may extend to ordinary nouns and forms referring to the speaker or listener.

A reported Qurʾānic interpretation from al-Zamakhsharī and a grammatical discussion from Abū Shāma are adduced in the continuation, showing that expressions with generic overt forms can carry an implicitly particular reference. Frequency of such a construction does not establish that it is exclusive.

### Editorial/source qualification

Crosses p158→159 and includes reports from earlier authors. Source-text full Arabic still to be collated and separately attributed.

<a id="hwn-117"></a>
## Note 117 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [159](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/159?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله أو تفهيمه للغير

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The words “or his giving understanding to another” mean his teaching that person. Here the disjunction aw need not assert that just one of the two possibilities holds; it can rule out the absence of both. The book may furnish insight both to the learner and to the teacher: one can read the preface as saying that the book assists a person seeking understanding in a context of imparting knowledge, whether that person is teaching another or being instructed by another.

A less expansive reading follows what is normally apparent: the work functions as an aid to one of these, and most naturally to the learner. This may explain why the principal commentator begins from the learner's perspective. The difference is between an exclusive alternative and an inclusive pair of possibilities, not two entirely different meanings of “insight.”

### Editorial/source qualification

Tracks disjunction as māniʿ al-khulūw, allowing both branches; voice requires printed signature control.

<a id="hwn-118"></a>
## Note 118 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [159](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/159?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله أو متعلق بيتذكر

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The prepositional expression may be an adverbial predicate (ẓarf mustaqarr) attached to a suppressed verb and functioning as a circumstantial qualifier of the subject of yatadhakkar (“he takes heed”). The subject is the implicit pronoun referring back to “whoever” in the relative expression. The underlying sense would be that the book was made a reminder for whoever wishes to recollect, *being among those who possess understanding*.

Alternatively the expression may be an ordinary adjunct (ẓarf laghw) directly governed by yatadhakkar. To make that latter construction semantically appropriate the commentator adds an implied sense of taking or learning. The mere verb “take heed” does not normally take the particular preposition min in the required manner, but a broader meaning of “take [knowledge] from” or “learn from” makes the link intelligible.

This is an example of taḍmīn, semantic inclusion: the overt verb retains its own central meaning while also allowing a closely related second verb to be understood, as indicated by the preposition appropriate to the second. One can make the overt verb primary and the implied sense subsidiary, or invert the priority. The glossator compares other expressions in learned introductions, such as “I arranged it according to such-and-such,” and commentary on Ibn al-Ḥājib's use of a phrase containing an atypical preposition.

### Editorial/source qualification

Clarifies that an apparent syntactic anomaly can reflect an intentionally included additional verbal meaning.

## Note 119 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [159](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/159?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله هذا أيضا يحتمل الوجهين

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The expression “this too admits two interpretations” parallels the preceding discussion of the phrase “when giving understanding.” The description “among the people of understanding” can describe the teacher if it is a predicative circumstantial expression, or the learner if it is directly attached to “take heed” by the semantic inclusion just explained.

That is the ordinary explanation. Yet the first construction also permits one to describe the learner as possessing understanding, so the opposition is not logically exhaustive. The reading preferred on contextual grounds remains the first, but it should not be transformed into a claim that the rival referent is impossible.

### Editorial/source qualification

Later gloss distinguishes grammatical possibility from interpretive preference.

<a id="hwn-120"></a>
## Note 120 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [159](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/159?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

السِّيُّ بالتشديد وقد يخفف

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The word siyy is pronounced with a doubled final consonant, although lighter pronunciations are attested, including in expressions with lā siyamā (“especially”). Its historical root is explained through the idea of equality or likeness, with an underlying wāw in some forms. When it is put into the dual, a separate genitive complement can sometimes be dispensed with, just as one may use the dual of mithl (“like, equal”) without an additional genitive expression. Examples are cited where two evils are called alike. The dual itself can supply the sense of the two corresponding terms; forming a similarly regular dual of sawāʾ is said to be rare.

### Editorial/source qualification

Traditional morphology of siyy and its dialectal reduction, not independently collated against earliest lexical authorities.

<a id="hwn-121"></a>
## Note 121 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [159](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/159?lang=ar) · [160](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/160?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī; reports Thaʿlab and al-Raḍī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله وأصل سيما لا سيما حذفت لا في اللفظ

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On the remark that the original expression was lā siyamā and that lā was subsequently dropped in speech: Thaʿlab is reported to have disapproved uses of the phrase that depart from an established poetic example employing “wa-lā siyamā.” His explanation is that lā siyamā has become a set expression introducing a matter more important or more highly ranked than what preceded. For example, “Charity is recommended in Ramadan, especially in its final ten nights” says that the recommendation becomes stronger in those nights than in Ramadan generally.

If one omitted lā and said only siyamā, he argues, the word would retain its ordinary sense of likeness or equivalence. The sentence would then compare the recommendation in Ramadan with that in the final ten nights rather than emphasizing the superiority of the latter.

But this reason establishes at most that the negative particle must be part of the interpretation—either pronounced or understood. It does not prove that the particle must always be uttered. Al-Raḍī notes that frequency of use has led to several altered forms: the expression occurs with lā omitted, and the yāʾ can be lightened with or without lā. Accordingly a linguistic argument for an underlying negative meaning should not be confused with proof that every conventional surface form must pronounce lā.

### Editorial/source qualification

Cross-page reading preserves Thaʿlab's prescriptive position and a specific objection based on al-Raḍī's usage evidence.

## Note 122 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [160](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/160?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb, p.12 (quoted). **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله هذا أصله ثم استعمل بمعنى خصوصا

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The statement that lā siyamā originally had one structure and was then used to mean “especially” does not require positing a wholly new sense. Its ordinary meaning and its idiomatic employment can be explained on one underlying account: the force of “especially this” is already implicit in saying that nothing is comparable to this case within the relevant set. The usage need not be treated as a distinct transferred meaning absent from its original composition.

### Editorial/source qualification

Source attribution to the quoted al-Taqrīb, not the main author.

<a id="hwn-123"></a>
## Note 123 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [160](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/160?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله وفيما بعده ثلاثة أوجه

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The word that follows lā siyamā can appear in three principal grammatical cases. In the genitive it is treated as the complement of siyy, while mā between them is regarded as an extra particle, comparable to expressions such as “which of the two appointed terms.” In the nominative the noun can be a predicate of an omitted subject; mā may then be interpreted as a relative pronoun whose clause is partly suppressed, or as an indefinite modified by an understood clause. This gives something like “there is no equal to that which is the most beloved son” or “to a thing which is the most beloved son.”

The genitive analysis is preferred to the nominative by some, because suppressing the beginning of a relative or adjectival clause is unusual. The nominative proposal can also require taking mā, ordinarily associated with nonrational referents, as referring to a rational person; this is controversial. Details of the vocalisation of siyy and its relation to the suppressed construction depend on the adopted parsing.

For the accusative there are again alternatives. One may posit an omitted verb “I mean,” making the following noun an object of specification, or treat it as a specification (tamyīz) when indefinite, comparable to an accusative of specification after mithl. In that interpretation mā prevents the genitive construction from operating. A further proposal analyses the phrase as an exception; critics argue that the element following the phrase is often expressly preferred rather than *excluded*. The reply is that it can be excluded from the equality suggested by the previous clause—a disconnected exception rather than removal from the subject class.

The coordinating wāw presents another difficulty for an exception reading, since one would not ordinarily say “the people came, and except Zayd” in that manner. Some answer that the entire idiom, with its variant pronunciations, has acquired a conventional force comparable to an exceptive construction. The glossator finally warns that this debate does not justify declaring the accusative impossible whenever the following noun is definite.

### Editorial/source qualification

A detailed first-pass translation of the three case patterns; individual reported examples and disputed phraseology still require a manuscript-controlled syntax review.

<a id="hwn-126"></a>
## Note 126 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [160](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/160?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

ثم السجع في اللغة هدير الحمام

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The preceding indication of restriction does not prevent a statement from having several different reasons or rhetorical effects: multiple causal and explanatory considerations can coexist.

Sajʿ lexically denotes the cooing or repeated call of a dove and similar sounds. A poetic line is quoted addressing a dove and inviting it to coo while in hearing of Suʿād. Technically, sajʿ in prose is the agreement of the terminal members of two clauses in their final sound or letter. The term may also be applied to the final word itself insofar as it agrees with the ending of a neighbouring clause.

Thus when one says “sajʿ in prose is like qāfiya in poetry,” the comparison concerns the terminal element, not merely an abstract agreement between any two final words; the strictly relational feature is more closely associated with the operation of making the ends rhyme. In practice the pairing of prose endings may require a pause and suppression of the final case ending, hence the rule that balanced prose endings are often read with sukun at their closures.

Some scholars dislike calling the recurrent terminations of Qurʾānic verses sajʿ, both because of the word's ordinary association with the dove's call and because sacred discourse should not be classified under an inapt literary label. They prefer fawāṣil, “verse endings,” and speak of the heads of verses. The rhetorical phenomenon and the choice of a respectful technical designation should therefore be distinguished.

### Editorial/source qualification

The verse, original technical definition and decorum claim belong to the glossator's cited tradition; no independent literary-critical attribution verified.

وقيل هو ترك السعي فيما لا يسعه قدرة البشر

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

A further account of tawakkul (reliance on God) says that one refrains from striving in matters that lie outside the scope of human ability. One employs the appropriate *cause* or means, yet does not imagine that the effect is produced by oneself independently. The speaker adduces the well-known reported instruction, “Tie up your camel and place your trust in God.” Reliance therefore need not exclude the practical use of causes. Rather, it excludes claiming independent control over the outcome.

### Editorial/source qualification

Short source note ends with Muḥammad ʿAlī's signature. The cited hadith is reported by the glossator; its authentication is not established by this translation.

<a id="hwn-128"></a>
## Note 128 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [161](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/161?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb, printed p.13 (reported). **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

هذا معناه العرفاني لا اللغوي

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

That is the spiritual or gnostic meaning of tawakkul, not its basic lexical meaning. In language, tawakkul denotes relying upon a thing or person, placing confidence in it, or treating it as what one depends upon: “he relied on him” means “he counted upon him and depended on him.” The technical spiritual account may therefore be narrower and richer than the ordinary dictionary sense.

### Editorial/source qualification

Quoted al-Taqrīb gloss, a separate voice from preceding Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī.

<a id="hwn-129"></a>
## Note 129 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [161](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/161?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

قوله لما علم ضمنا

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

On “since it was understood implicitly”: the particle lammā may be treated as an adverbial of time, or as a particle comparable to idh. It links the occurrence of the content of one sentence with the occurrence of another. In “when Zayd arrived, ʿAmr arrived,” those treating lammā as a particle may express the first occurrence as explanatory of the second, while those treating it as a temporal adverb assert only their simultaneity or conjunction in time, whether the two events are causally related or merely coincide.

The point of this discussion is to explain the indefinite form of muqaddima (“an introduction”) and the definite form of the two divisions of the book. Adding the definite article is not an unmotivated act: it requires a reason. The two divisions have already been referred to, at least implicitly, and can therefore be named as known; the introduction has not been introduced in the same way and remains indefinite. The first division is accordingly called *the* first division.

One objection says that the absence of a previously mentioned referent does not require an indefinite expression. The definite article can also indicate a familiar external referent or other types of definiteness. The initial explanation is therefore incomplete if it implies that prior verbal mention is the only possible ground of definition. Moreover external familiarity can single out a determinate object even where it was not previously named in speech. The order of exposition also has a purpose: the author begins with logic because that first division is preparatory to the second, theology.

### Editorial/source qualification

The reported temporal-vs-causal semantics of lammā are historical grammarians' analyses, not a complete formal semantics. Source Q161 is long but one numbered gloss.

---

<a id="hwn-130"></a>
## Note 130 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [161](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/161?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

اعلم أن لما هذه تختص بالماضي

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The lammā under discussion is especially connected with past occurrences and requires two clauses, the second happening in relation to the first, as in “when he invited me, I responded.” Grammarians disagree whether it is a particle or an adverb. Among the particle analyses there are further disputes over whether it links one event's existence to the existence of another or makes one obligation follow another. Those who call it an adverb differ over its correspondence to ḥīn (“when”) or idh (“at the time when”).

Ibn Khurūf reportedly objects to calling lammā an adverb, since a person may say “when you honoured me yesterday, I honoured you today.” If the verb in the consequent governs lammā as an adverb, it seems that one verb would have to occupy two different times. The reply distinguishes the time of the act from the time of the fact's becoming established: one may understand “when it was established today that you had honoured me yesterday, I honoured you.” The source compares this with the Qurʾānic construction “If I had said it, You would have known it,” whose relevant conditional relation need not assign the two acts the same time.

This also answers an objection about the author's organisation. The expression “the first division” normally follows an explicit division into two or more parts; the writer had not previously stated a numbered table of contents. Yet he had already referred to the refinement of discourse in *logic and theology*, which indicates the two parts sufficiently for a subsequent mention of “the first division.”

The principal gloss then distinguishes why that first division is definite but the introduction is indefinite. Some take that distinction to be the whole aim of the comment, rather than a subsidiary consequence of establishing the implied division. The present glossator replies that this fails to explain the wording and the explanatory connective in the principal gloss. If the definite/indefinite contrast alone were the primary goal, a different past-tense formulation without the fāʾ would have been stylistically expected.

### Editorial/source qualification

Long source note ends in Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī signature; one first-pass English reading, not photographed and not modern-modal formal proof.

<a id="hwn-132"></a>
## Note 132 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [162](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/162?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

ربما يتوهم ان هذا انما يقتضى انتفاء تعريفها بلام العهد

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

An objector might say that the preceding reasoning eliminates at most the definite article of prior mention (*lām al-ʿahd al-dhikrī*). It does not prove that every type of definiteness is impossible, since the absence of one special class does not imply the absence of the entire general class.

The first reply is contextual. The author was contrasting this introduction with the first division, which was definite by virtue of having been mentioned. For that limited contrast it suffices to identify the missing ground of definiteness. A second reply appeals to the other possible readings: in this location neither universal comprehensiveness, a familiar technical introduction, quiddity considered simply as such, nor an unspecified individual instance would be suitable. Since the alternatives are contextually unavailable, the particular reason of previous mention can be cited without rehearsing the others.

The glossator clarifies the form of the objection: the proposed conclusion seems broader than the evidence, which excludes only one member of the class. He suggests two ways to restore a proper relation between evidence and conclusion. Either restrict what the conclusion itself is claiming, or treat the stated evidence as a conspicuous part of a larger argument whose remaining premises are sufficiently obvious to have been omitted.

### Editorial/source qualification

Contains quotation and subsequent explanation signed by Muḥammad ʿAlī; excludes a formal logical error only under the contextual restriction made explicit.

<a id="hwn-133"></a>
## Note 133 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [162](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/162?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb, printed p.13 (quoted). **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

ليس المراد بالقسم الأول الا المسائل المنطقية

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

An objection says that “the first division” simply means the questions of logic. If “logic” means those same questions, the two expressions differ only in their wording, not their referent. One cannot literally place something *in* itself merely by changing its name: a contained thing and its container must be distinct.

The principal commentary answers by distinguishing a book's title from what is titled. The “first division” may designate the heading or organized literary part, while logic denotes the discipline's subject-matter. Those are different enough for a relation of inclusion. The quoted marginal critic finds the remaining elaborate explanations unnecessary: they distinguish words, meanings, graphic inscriptions, pairwise combinations, triple combinations, acquired logical disposition, the science of all logical problems or a substantial body of them, and the problems themselves. In his judgment this enumeration mostly multiplies expressions without advancing the explanation.

### Editorial/source qualification

A substantial editorial criticism from al-Taqrīb, not an agreed conclusion of the principal commentary.

<a id="hwn-134"></a>
## Note 134 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [162](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/162?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

يلزم اتحاد الظرف والمظروف

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The objection is that the contained and the container would become identical, which is impossible for a literal relation of containment. A thing cannot contain itself in exactly the same respect in which it is contained. A different conceptual consideration or a genuinely different referent is required if the wording is to be defended.

### Editorial/source qualification

One-sentence categorical distinction; later notes qualify the issue for metaphorical containment.
