<a id="hwn-135"></a>
## Note 135 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [162](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/162?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

يجوز ان يراد بالقسم الأول

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Most of the proposed ways of interpreting containment can be made intelligible. One exception occurs when “the first division” means the meanings of the logical propositions and “logic” means the propositions themselves. If the propositions are just those meanings, the phrase reduces to “these meanings in these meanings,” and the objection of self-containment returns.

An attempted solution says that “the first division” denotes a universal whose only instance lies in logic, so the sense would be “this universal is in this particular.” A critic regards that as contrived. Another solution treats the first division as a part of logic, because the discipline is not exhausted by the questions in this literary division. The intended relation would then be “this particular part is in the wider whole.” Such a part–whole relation is clearer than making a universal literally present inside its own single instance.

### Editorial/source qualification

Reports competing resolutions of the same container/contained argument; the part–whole reading is not automatically valid if 'logic' means only exactly the same selected problems.

<a id="hwn-137"></a>
## Note 137 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [163](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/163?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

والمنطق عبارة عن أحد معان خمسة اما الملكة

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The word malaka (“acquired disposition”) denotes a quality that has become settled so firmly in the person who possesses it that it cannot be readily removed at will. If a quality does not yet have that stability and the person can easily rid himself of it, it is called a ḥāl (“state”). This understanding of malaka is one of the common senses in which knowledge is spoken of. The glossator wonders why a particular marginal commentator on al-Muṭawwal did not include it.

A further objection concerns the familiar metaphor that expressions are the moulds of meanings. If a book or its division is treated as verbal expressions *contained in* meanings, while meanings themselves are treated as *contained in* verbal expressions, does each become both container and contained by the other?

The reply distinguishes standpoints. The “container” of words can be their *explanatory function*: expressions are arranged to disclose meanings, which may also be disclosed without those particular expressions. In that respect the work of explaining meanings provides a governing framework for the expressions. Conversely verbal expressions can be described as moulds of meanings because the understanding obtained from them expands or contracts with the expressions, and meanings are received through their formulation.

It is impossible for one and the same thing to be container and contained of itself *in the same respect* under the same literal relation. But the two descriptions here concern different relations and are partly metaphorical, so the alleged contradiction does not follow.

### Editorial/source qualification

Malaka vs ḥāl and two distinct 'container' respects. Speaker ends with ʿAbd al-Raḥīm signature.

## Note 138 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [163](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/163?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb, printed p.13 (quoted). **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

العلم بجميع المسائل أو نفس المسائل

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

The difference between “knowing all the questions” and “the questions themselves” is that in the first case the questions are considered under the illumination of being known or disclosed, while in the second they are considered simply as questions in themselves, without considering their disclosure to any particular knower. The same contents are thus distinguished by whether their epistemic relation is included in the conception.

### Editorial/source qualification

Quoted al-Taqrīb note distinguishes knowledge-as-disclosure from known propositions considered apart from their epistemic status.

<a id="hwn-139"></a>
## Note 139 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source page:** [163](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/163?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī. **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

فيحصل من ملاحظة الخمسة

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Most combinations of the seven possible referents of “the first division” and five possible meanings of “logic” can be interpreted straightforwardly. A problematic class remains where the first division is taken as meanings, either alone or combined with another element, while logic is taken as the problems themselves, whether all of them or a substantial selection. The problems themselves are those meanings. The result then seems to be “meanings are in meanings,” merely returning to the objection the explanation was supposed to escape.

One answer distinguishes the division from the whole discipline: the first division comprises only a part of logic, since not every possible logical question appears in it. Therefore a particular subset can be said to be within the wider class. But this does not solve the case where “logic” denotes only the substantial body of problems actually in the very same division; then the division is not a proper part of that body but the same thing.

Another proposed answer distinguishes unrestricted meanings from meanings of a special kind. On this reading one says in effect that the meanings of the book serve to explain this particular kind of meaning, namely logical meanings. This is compatible with the earlier restriction to specific words and meanings, the glossator says, when each statement is taken under its proper conceptual aspect. Merely considering a thing under an aspect does not bring into existence a second independent thing.

### Editorial/source qualification

Source explicitly rejects a first attempted part–whole repair in the narrow case and distinguishes conceptual consideration from numerically distinct things.

<a id="hwn-140"></a>
## Note 140 — first-pass English COMPLETE

**Section:** H preface `HWN`. **Qum source pages:** [163](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/163?lang=ar) · [164](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/164?lang=ar). **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb, printed p.13 (quoted). **Arabic:** Short locator lemma ONLY — historical Arabic not fully transcribed. **Photo or manuscript certified:** NO.

### Arabic locator lemma

<div dir="rtl" lang="ar">

يقدر في بعضها

</div>

### English working first-pass rendering

Even when one of the thirty-five combinations—seven possible things meant by the first division multiplied by five possible senses of logic—has been given a formally defensible container/contained relation, one further requirement remains. The actual words must yield an idiomatic and intellectually appropriate sentence.

For example, “the words in the disposition” may avoid the strongest logical objection to containment, but it does not read naturally in the present literary construction. One should instead speak of “words *for acquiring* the disposition,” “words *for explaining* the meanings,” or “words *for attaining* the body of problems.” The supplied verbal noun—explanation, acquisition or attainment—establishes the correct relation between the otherwise mismatched terms.

This corrective procedure must be performed for each of the thirty-five theoretical combinations rather than assuming that, once an abstract relation of inclusion has been proved possible, the author's wording is already stylistically appropriate. The point is the difference between **logical coherence of a proposed relation** and **idiomatic suitability of its linguistic expression**.

### Editorial/source qualification

Q163 source sentence continues to the end of Q164 and closes before a NEW textual heading at Q165. The next printed page begins 'حواشي مقدمة علم المنطق' and RESTARTS local gloss numbering at (1); do not continue with a false note141 from that new section.

<a id="hwn-intro-001"></a>
## Introduction note 1 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-001` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.165](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/165?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله أي هذه مقدمة

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

On the explanation “that is, this is an introduction”: the word *muqaddima* is a predicate of an omitted demonstrative subject, “this.” This follows the basic order that the subject is definite and the predicate indefinite. Others instead make “an introduction” the subject, with an omitted predicate: “an introduction [is concerned] with defining logic, explaining the need for it, and stating its subject.” An objection arises that a completely indefinite noun cannot ordinarily serve as the subject of a statement. Several replies are given. One can treat the indefiniteness as specially qualified by the tanwīn of magnification—signalling the many benefits of this introduction—or of diminution—signalling the brevity of its expressions. Alternatively place the omitted predicate before it: “In the definition of logic, the need for it, and its subject, there is an introduction,” like “In the house is a man.” A more general response judges the admissibility of an indefinite subject by whether the sentence conveys information, not by a fixed list of formal qualifications. Thus “A tree prostrated” and “A star fell just now” can inform the hearer, whereas a bare “A man is standing” may lack informative force in the relevant context.

A further objection targets the original analysis “this is an introduction.” Does the demonstrative not designate the three matters mentioned—the definition, need, and subject—whereas the introduction is not identical with those matters but is an exposition *about* them? Even granting this distinction, one need not take “this” to point to the three items themselves; it can point instead to the specifically arranged words and meanings that introduce them. The same response is available to those who understand the suppressed subject as “these matters.”

### Editorial qualifications

Entire p165 numbered note1 in first pass; the objection concerns reference and identity, not just definite-vs-indefinite forms.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-002"></a>
## Introduction note 2 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-002` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī, with separate reported ʿAbd al-Raḥīm comment · **Original printed source:** [p.165](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/165?lang=ar) · [p.166](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/166?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله يتبين فيها أمور ثلاثة

</div>

### Working English (partial)

The words “three matters are explained in it” raise a problem about literal containment. The treatment is comparable to the prior discussion of “the first division is in logic”: a thing and what is contained in it must not be identical in precisely the same respect. One proposal, connected with al-Taftāzānī's commentary on the *Talkhīṣ*, distinguishes the *introduction to a science* from the *introduction to a book*. The former comprises what must be known in order to embark intelligently on the discipline—its definition, end and subject. The latter is the portion of a writer's discourse placed ahead of the principal subject because it is connected to and useful for that subject, whether or not the science strictly depends on it. The former could then be said to contain the latter, avoiding literal self-containment. Another proposal considers the three preliminary matters themselves as parts of the introduction, as one says that a particular inquiry occurs “in” a particular chapter.

A reported comment from ʿAbd al-Raḥīm challenges the supposition that “introduction to a book” is necessarily a new technical expression unsupported by usage. Writers regularly name their leading portions introductions, chapters, sections, *namṭ*, *tanbīh* or *ishāra*, and such designations cannot be confined to one prescribed vocabulary. The source compares an introduction with the advance detachment of an army, from which “what goes before” can be extended to a beginning. A further response by Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī distinguishes two aspects: the referent of “introduction” in the two analyses (science vs book), and whether containment applies separately to each of the three topics or to the topics taken collectively.

### Editorial qualifications

The whole p165–166 dossier combines signed prose by different voices; this note is an analytic first pass, NOT full literal transcription of each quote. Keep incomplete pending clause/signature collation.

<a id="hwn-intro-007"></a>
## Introduction note 7 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-007` · **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb p.14, quoting al-Khaṭṭāʾī; separate ʿAbd al-Raḥīm and Muḥammad ʿAlī comments · **Original printed source:** [p.166](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/166?lang=ar) · [p.167](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/167?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

وهي مأخوذة من مقدمة الجيش

</div>

### Working English (partial)

On “it is derived from the vanguard of an army”: *muqaddima* is the group that proceeds before the rest of the army. Transferring the term to an introductory section of a book may be a new technical assignment, making it literally applicable in that convention, or a metaphor, like calling a brave man a lion. The likeness is that both are portions of a larger whole placed before the remainder.

Al-Khaṭṭāʾī suggests another possibility: the term was originally an adjective whose qualified noun was omitted, then used for a group of meanings or words which precedes the science or the rest of the book. The feminine ending may signal the transfer from adjectival to nominal use, or correspond to an implicitly feminine noun such as *ṭāʾifa* (“group”). Whether the resulting use is literal or figurative depends on whether the adjective is considered a general property of being advanced or a noun conventionally assigned to a specific kind of advance detachment.

The following, separately introduced ʿAbd al-Raḥīm passage compares the meanings of *muqaddima* with kasra or fatḥa on the dāl. The common pronunciation with kasra looks like an active participle; however the author's act of putting a section first might suggest a passive participle. A response takes the base verb in an intransitive sense, “to go forward,” making the preceding material properly active in advancing; another considers the active form as indicating that the section, by enabling insight, effectively puts itself forward. That answer faces an objection from the usual rule that the intensive causative form is transitive, though the rule may be predominant rather than without exceptions.

### Editorial qualifications

Substantial cross-page translation selection; the source includes multiple separately introduced voices and examples requiring full line-by-line collation, so remain partial.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-008"></a>
## Introduction note 8 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-008` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.167](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/167?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

إشارة إلى أن المقدمة يطلق على معنيين آخرين

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The commentator signals that *muqaddima* also has two other uses. First, it is a proposition made a component of a syllogism or other proof. Second, it is something on which the validity of an argument depends—for example, in the first syllogistic figure, the minor premise's being affirmative and the major premise's being universal. The second use is broader than the first, since it includes conditions of an inference as well as its constituent propositions.

### Editorial qualifications

The two logical senses of premise are distinct from the earlier book/science introduction distinction.

<a id="hwn-intro-009"></a>
## Introduction note 9 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-009` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.167](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/167?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

والمراد منها هاهنا

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The words “what is intended by it here” specify that this occurrence means *the introduction to the book*, not the introduction to the science, as the term is used in some other works. The glossator has already distinguished them and directs the reader back to that discussion.

The definition of the present introduction as a “portion of discourse” is the definition of a book's introduction. That is why the author does not say that the science's problems necessarily depend on it, as one would when defining a science's introduction. The small word “here” is thus significant. Al-Sayyid al-Sharīf makes an analogous observation: in syllogistic reasoning the word “premise” means either a proposition entering the proof or something on which its soundness depends, including conditions such as affirmative minor, its actuality and universal major in the first figure.

### Editorial qualifications

Source note ends Q167 before numbered 10. Preserve al-Sharīf as a quoted authority.

<a id="hwn-intro-010"></a>
## Introduction note 10 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-010` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī, with separately cited al-Taqrīb · **Original printed source:** [p.167](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/167?lang=ar) · [p.168](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/168?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

إن كان الكتاب عبارة عن الألفاظ

</div>

### Working English (partial)

If “book” means its expressions, its “introduction” is a portion of the book's words placed before the principal discourse. If “book” refers to the meanings signified, the introduction may denote knowledge on which the science depends. The source criticizes proliferating word–meaning distinctions which obscure the genuine difference between the preliminary material necessary for a science and a literary introduction which assists comprehension without constituting that discipline.

The glossator rejects making the introduction to a science depend entirely on the author's act of ordering words. Some preliminary investigations, including the definition of a science, its subject and its need or purpose, are intellectual foundations of the discipline; introductory discussion of technical terminology can instead be indispensable merely to understanding a particular book's expressions. The long continuation is concerned with whether a philosophical introduction must itself be a constituent of the science and whether distinguishing the two introductions produces a useful explanation.

### Editorial qualifications

Long numbered note extends across Q167–168 and contains a separate source citation. Rendered as selected argument, not certified complete translation; remains PARTIAL.

<a id="hwn-intro-011"></a>
## Introduction note 11 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-011` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī (after al-Taqrīb citation) · **Original printed source:** [p.168](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/168?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

أي من الكلام اللفظي كما هو ظاهر

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

This means “from verbal discourse,” as is clear from the context. A *ṭāʾifa* is a group or portion. Some authorities put its minimum at three or four, others at two or three. Its form is among adjectives that have come to be used substantively, as though the original expression were “a group which separates itself from something.”

It is reported from Mujāhid and Ibn ʿAbbās, however, that a *ṭāʾifa* may denote even one person or more. This accords with al-Jawharī's general explanation of it as a “piece of something.” Qurʾān 9:122, which speaks of a party going forth from every group to acquire religious understanding, is interpreted as allowing a single person, or a pair, to comprise the party. The plural pronoun in the following words does not require each individual *ṭāʾifa* to contain a plurality; it can refer to the multiple parties collectively conveyed by the context.

### Editorial qualifications

Lexical reports and Qurʾānic exegesis are the glossator's cited authorities, not independently authenticated interpretations.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-012"></a>
## Introduction note 12 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-012` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.168](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/168?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

لا يخفى أن ارتباط المقصود إنما هو بمعاني تلك الطائفة

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

It is obvious that the principal topic is connected to the *meanings* conveyed by that group of expressions, not to the expressions considered merely as sounds or letters. Likewise, what is beneficial for the subject is the meanings, not the words simply as words. Hence one may supply an omitted genitive: “because the purpose is connected with *their meanings*, and *their meanings* are beneficial to it.”

Alternatively no omission is needed. Since expressions are the means through which knowledge is communicated and received, the connection and benefit are attributed to them figuratively. On the first analysis there is figurative omission (*majāz al-ḥadhf*), and on the second there is figurative attribution (*majāz fī al-isnād*).

### Editorial qualifications

Clearly distinguishes ellipsis from attribution to instruments of teaching.

<a id="hwn-intro-015"></a>
## Introduction note 15 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-015` · **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb pp.14–15 (quoted) · **Original printed source:** [p.168](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/168?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله وتجويز الاحتمالات الأخر في الكتاب

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The author says “An introduction concerning the definition of logic, the need for it and its subject.” Yet if the *introduction* is itself simply those three matters, how can one say the three matters are *in* it without making container and contained identical?

Any answer available for the earlier expression “the first division is in logic” is also available here, since the same problem arises. But the commentators do not rehearse all seven possible referents of a book's division and the five possible meanings of logic. Rather they say that “introduction” refers to expressions, while the three matters refer to meanings: the expressions serve to explain those meanings. The present critic asks why the many alternative repairs were enumerated for the previous case but not for this one, since the original motive for the enumeration has not disappeared.

### Editorial qualifications

Quoted critical question about inconsistent prolixity of the commentary; not endorsed as the sole proper resolution.

<a id="hwn-intro-018"></a>
## Introduction note 18 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-018` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī; later al-Karmī passage separately quoted · **Original printed source:** [p.168](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/168?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله هو الصورة الحاصلة

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

On “knowledge is the form that comes to be [in the intellect]”: some define knowledge as the soul's reception of such a form, and others as the form of the object coming to be present in the intellect. On the first interpretation it is assigned to the category of quality; on the second, to that of passion or reception; on the third, as some analyse the cognitive relation, to the category of relation. This is not the place for a full decision among these philosophical accounts; the issue belongs in extended works of philosophy.

### Editorial qualifications

Short printed note18 closes at the source signature. A subsequent much later quotation from Shaykh Muḥammad al-Karmī criticises formal definitions of knowledge; keep it separately attributed and NOT as note18.

### Adjacent UNNUMBERED quoted gloss — separate source voice, not additional numbered note

**Reported attribution:** Shaykh Muḥammad al-Karmī, a later separate quoted intervention (excerpt). Its Arabic here is a short locator/excerpt, NOT a full Arabic transcription; the English is a selected explanation and is NOT an authenticated historical quotation.

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

أنا لم أجد تعريفا للعلم ... حقيقة العلم انكشاف الواقع

</div>

The later cited author criticizes the definitions of knowledge that reduce it to the mere occurrence of a form, favouring the disclosure of things as they actually are. A representation can seem to reflect reality while in fact deceiving the knower. Thus a good definition must distinguish truthful disclosure from imagined or illusory presentation. This is a selected translation of the quoted objection, NOT the whole modern insertion; its speaker/date must be registered separately from the earlier Arabic gloss.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-019"></a>
## Introduction note 19 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-019` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī, following separate quoted al-Taqrīb discussion · **Original printed source:** [p.169](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/169?lang=ar) · [p.170](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/170?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله والمصنف لم يتعرض بتعريفه

</div>

### Working English (partial)

Why did Taftāzānī divide knowledge into conception and assent before defining knowledge itself? Division is a judgment about a thing; one might expect the thing to be understood before judgments are made about it. Three explanations are offered. First, a preliminary conception “in some respect” suffices for division: one need only recognise the familiar use of “knowledge” in the discipline. Grammarians similarly divide an exception into connected and disconnected kinds without first supplying an exhaustive definition of the exceptional term. Second, knowledge's definition is already familiar, so the author omits it for brevity. Third, the concept of knowledge is self-evident through experience, as al-Rāzī is reported to hold. Knowledge is among the qualities apprehended by introspection, like hunger, thirst and satiety.

A further argument appeals to ordinary speakers: even people untrained in logical definition, when asked “Do you know this?”, readily answer yes or no. They must therefore grasp the concept of knowledge well enough to understand the question. The glossator objects that neither introspection nor such answers proves that the *word* ʿilm has a self-evident lexical signification. One may immediately encounter an instance or grasp the general concept while still requiring an explanation that this word has been assigned to it.

An attempted defence distinguishes real definition from verbal explanation. The cited arguments might establish that one does not require a real definition of knowledge, while leaving lexical explanation open. The objection to the common-speaker argument also turns on whether the listener already knows the word's conventional assignment. If he does, he may answer without knowing the full real nature; if he does not, he cannot grasp the question merely by hearing it. The author later recognises that the arguments were perhaps directed only toward the impossibility or unnecessary character of a *real* definition, not a verbal gloss.

A further objection says knowledge cannot be defined at all: we know everything by knowledge, so knowing knowledge through something else would be circular. One answer is that knowing an object does not require explicit knowledge of the linguistic assignment of ʿilm. Another distinguishes the universal cognition which is supposedly to be defined from the particular cognitions through which we apprehend things; the supposed circle then equivocates on “knowledge.” The discussion ends by asking why Taftāzānī mentioned assent before conception, though conception is prior in nature as either a part or condition of assent: the answer invokes the greater dignity of assent's positive character compared with conception understood privatively.

### Editorial qualifications

Long source note extends Q169–170 with intertwined authorial objections and replies. This is a substantive selected first pass, not a claim of full clause-by-clause translation. Leave PARTIAL pending source/voice collation.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-020"></a>
## Introduction note 20 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-020` · **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb pp.15, critical anonymous voice · **Original printed source:** [p.170](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/170?lang=ar) · [p.171](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/171?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله العلم إن كان إذعانا للنسبة فتصديق وإلا فتصور

</div>

### Working English (partial)

The author's words divide knowledge as follows: if it is an assent (*idhʿān*) to a relation, it is *taṣdīq*; otherwise it is *taṣawwur*. A critical commentator objects to allowing all other mental states into the latter category. He argues that genuine knowledge is disclosure of things as they are. When an affirmative or negative predicative relation between a subject and predicate is genuinely disclosed and the soul recognises and accepts that relation, the resulting act is assent. If the disclosure occurs without acceptance, the commentator describes a refusal or resistance, not an additional piece of genuine disclosed knowledge. When an isolated individual, scattered individuals, or an incomplete relation is made present—“Zayd,” “ʿAmr and Khālid,” “Zayd's servant”—we instead have conception, the presentation of their forms.

On this view it is a mistake to count performative or constructive sentences as conceptions of objective reality, since an *inshāʾ* brings about what is said rather than reporting an already existing predicative fact. The same critic rejects counting a complete assertoric relation entertained without assent—such as a fictional image, doubt or mere conjecture—as a true disclosure of reality merely because it is a mental presentation.

In his stronger formulation, assent is a condition or event of the soul, not a four-part compound of subject, predicate, relation and act of acceptance. The fact that these constituents can be described does not make the psychological act of assent simply their aggregate. The source polemically dismisses the compound theory; this is the critical writer's position rather than a claim that all historical logicians accepted it.

### Editorial qualifications

Long al-Taqrīb debate across Q170–171; chosen passages translated and argument mapped but not certified fully clause-by-clause. Author attribution and editorial insertion need control.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-021"></a>
## Introduction note 21 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-021` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.171](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/171?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

فسر النسبة الحكمية بالنسبة التامة الخبرية

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The “judgmental relation” (*nisba ḥukmiyya*) is explained as the complete reportable relation, affirmative in an affirmative proposition and negative in a negative one. Some instead maintain that the relation must be an affirmative restrictive relation in either case, lest the negative judgment turn into an affirmative assertion concerning nonoccurrence.

That objection works only if a restrictive relation is itself made the explicit subject of a further judgment—for example, “the relation of nonestablishment between the terms does not obtain.” It does not apply when one simply conceives the complete predicative relation expressed in “Zayd is not a writer” and then assents to it.

The correct analysis, the writer contends, treats the judgmental relation as complete and reportable. Philosophers agree that conceiving a judgmental relation precedes the occurrence of judgment. The relation must therefore be conceivably complete before assent is added. One can conceive a subject, predicate and complete predicative link without assenting and later assent without having separately entertained an additional restrictive relation between them. Introspection supports this distinction.

### Editorial qualifications

Contrast between complete assertoric relation and restrictive relation is the focus; avoid treating the writer's claim of philosophical unanimity as verified consensus.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-022"></a>
## Introduction note 22 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-022` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.171](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/171?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

اعلم أنهم اختلفوا في حقيقة التصديق

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

There are three views of the reality of *taṣdīq*. The philosophers say it is the very act of assent and judgment. Al-Rāzī and his followers treat it as a compound of four items: the conception of what is judged about (subject), of what is attributed (predicate), of the predicative relation between them, and assent. The author of *al-Kashf* and his followers treat it as the first three taken in the condition of judgment's accruing to them; this is known as the newer school.

Thus under the first view judgment is identical with assent; under the second it is one part of assent; under the third it is external to the three conceived elements and attaches to them as an accident. Taftāzānī selects the first when he writes “knowledge, if it is assent to a relation, is taṣdīq.” Further detail belongs in longer treatises, not this brief gloss.

### Editorial qualifications

Direct three-school map of taṣdīq: simple judgment; compound of four; three cognitions with judgment as accident.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-023"></a>
## Introduction note 23 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-023` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm (signature at Q173) · **Original printed source:** [p.171](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/171?lang=ar) · [p.172](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/172?lang=ar) · [p.173](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/173?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله فقد اختار المصنف مذهب الحكماء

</div>

### Working English (partial)

The assertion that Taftāzānī chose the philosophers' position requires distinguishing two different questions. The first concerns the *nature of assent* (*taṣdīq*). For the philosophers it is the act of judgment, an intellectual apprehension accompanied by assent toward a complete predicative relation. It differs from simple conception in kind, not merely in having a different object. For al-Rāzī and his followers it is a compound of four constituents—conception of subject, predicate and relation, together with the act of judgment. Conceiving the three is a prerequisite of simple assent on the first theory; it is a *part* of assent on the second.

The distinction is relevant to logic's purpose. Conceived unknowns are acquired through an explanatory statement (*qawl shāriḥ*), whereas the act of assent is acquired through a proof (*ḥujja*). If assent is a simple intellectual act, these two kinds of acquired knowledge can be distinguished according to their acquisition. A composite containing several conceptual elements acquired in one way and an additional judgment acquired in another does not as naturally admit one distinctive procedure of acquisition.

Some attempt to reconcile the views by saying that the same assent is simple when considered as a whole and composite upon analysis into its presupposed contents. But al-Rāzī expressly calls assent the aggregate of conceptions and judgment, and contrasts composite with simple in *al-Mulakhkhaṣ*. Treating his stated composition as merely a different way of viewing a simple act obscures that explicit disagreement.

The second question concerns the *constituents of a proposition*. The older philosophers are described as counting three: subject, predicate and complete affirmative or negative predicative relation. Later philosophers count four by adding the obtaining or nonobtaining of the positive restrictive relation, motivated by cases of doubt. One can conceive a relation without judging whether it obtains; the cognition of obtaining/nonobtaining appears to be an additional element.

An opposing account replies that the same relation can be conceived without assent and apprehended with assent, without multiplying components. Some later writers distinguish the two considerations of the same relation—conceived and judged—and explain the different counts as analytic rather than numerical. The author of *Sharḥ al-Maṭāliʿ* is cited for allowing four elements upon detailed analysis. Taftāzānī's wording, however, describes assent to *the complete predicative relation*, and his later treatment of categorical propositions appears to require three formal parts. The source considers whether a suppressed “occurrence of” could be supplied to restore four parts, but rejects that as an interpretation contrary to his explicit later exposition.

### Editorial qualifications

Extensive Q171–173 numbered argument rendered as structured partial English; full Arabic and speaker boundaries still require clause-level audit. Do not mark complete.

---

## Introduction note 24 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-024` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.173](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/173?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

الأولى أن يزيد عليه تصور النسبة الحكمية

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

It would have been better to add the conception of the predicative relation to the list. Al-Rāzī's position is that assent is compounded of *four* elements, not merely three, as already explained. Yet the commentator's goal here was not to investigate every constituent of al-Rāzī's position; it was to identify what distinguishes that view from the philosophers' account. The omission is therefore understandable.

### Editorial qualifications

Reports an editorial desideratum and its contextual excuse, not a change actually present in the matn.

<a id="hwn-intro-025"></a>
## Introduction note 25 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-025` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.173](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/173?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله واختار مذهب القدماء

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The philosophers agree that assent itself is judgment and is not a compound containing the three prior conceptions. They disagree, however, about the composition of the *proposition*. The earlier philosophers count three constituents: the subject, the predicate, and the complete affirmative or negative assertoric relation. Later philosophers add a fourth: the occurrence or nonoccurrence of that relation.

Taftāzānī chooses the former position, since he makes the object of assent the predicative relation itself, rather than a distinct “occurrence” or “nonoccurrence” of the relation as the later account would.

### Editorial qualifications

Distinguishes *the act of assent* from *the logical proposition*; source note ends before no.26.

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-028` · **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb p.16 (quoted) · **Original printed source:** [p.173](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/173?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

<a id="hwn-intro-029"></a>
## Introduction note 29 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-029` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī, followed by later separate al-Karmī quote · **Original printed source:** [p.173](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/173?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله لا وقوع النسبة الثبوتية التقييدية

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The restriction “occurrence or nonoccurrence of the positive restrictive relation” is awkward. It would be better to say “reportable relation” (*nisba khabariyya*) instead. Likewise, qualifying the predicative relation by “restrictive” adds no useful distinction on the reading assumed here, since, in the commentator's claim, the reportable relation already involves a restriction or predication.

### Editorial qualifications

A later separate comment by Shaykh Muḥammad al-Karmī on the different technical uses of 'restrictive' immediately follows and must not be counted as the numbered note29.

### Adjacent UNNUMBERED quoted gloss — separate source voice, not additional numbered note

**Reported attribution:** Shaykh Muḥammad al-Karmī, separate later printed quotation after note29. Its Arabic here is a short locator/excerpt, NOT a full Arabic transcription; the English is a selected explanation and is NOT an authenticated historical quotation.

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

تقييد النسب التامة بالتقييدية لغو

</div>

The late commentator criticizes the phrase “a complete restrictive relation”: a complete assertoric connection already predicates something of a subject, whereas “restrictive relation” is more normally reserved for incomplete relational or adjectival constructions. He therefore regards the added qualification as misleading. This is a separately quoted critical gloss, not an independently verified variant of the historical principal commentary.

<a id="hwn-intro-030"></a>
## Introduction note 30 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-030` · **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb pp.15–16 (quoted) · **Original printed source:** [p.173](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/173?lang=ar) · [p.174](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/174?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله وقوع النسبة أو لا وقوعها

</div>

### Working English (partial)

What precisely is meant by the “occurrence or nonoccurrence of the relation”? If it means the affirmative or negative form actually uttered in a proposition, or entertained inwardly as a mental proposition, it is simply another way of describing an affirmative or negative relation. It adds no genuinely new constituent. If instead “occurrence” means the proposition's actual establishment in reality, and “nonoccurrence” its absence there, that is a different matter from the affirmative or negative form of speech or imagination. A spoken or mentally conceived affirmative can disagree with reality, just as an uttered negative can be false.

The commentator insists that the soul's proper assent is directed toward a relation disclosed as affirmative or negative by reality. An assent made without such real disclosure may be nothing more than acceptance of an illusion or misleading presentation. One should therefore not assume that the mere occurrence of a positive/negative verbal or imagined form makes a further fourth proposition-component.

If “occurrence” is construed as the existence of the relation in reality, that does not straightforwardly support treating it as an additional *part of a proposition* either. It may be a relation between proposition and extramental state of affairs rather than a further constituent of the internally structured proposition. On the critic's premises, the older/later philosophers' disagreement over three or four components is therefore partly sustained by shifting the meaning of “occurrence” between linguistic, mental and actual senses.

### Editorial qualifications

Long printed Q173–174 al-Taqrīb critique; full argumentative English synopsis but some Arabic distinctions omitted, so PARTIAL.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-031"></a>
## Introduction note 31 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-031` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.174](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/174?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله وسيشير المصنف

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The words “the author will indicate” forestall an objection. His description of judgment as assent to the relation alone does not, merely on its face, prove he accepts the earlier philosophers' three-part proposition. One might supply a suppressed genitive and coordination: “assent to the occurrence or nonoccurrence of the relation.” Such omissions are not unknown in eloquent Arabic. For example, Qurʾān 16:81 mentions garments guarding against heat, from which cold may also be understood, and Qurʾān 87:9 commands reminding if it benefits, with a disputed understanding of the unmentioned contrary case.

The reply is that the author later explicitly treats the proposition as having three constituents. It would then be illegitimate to impose the four-part interpretation on this earlier wording, since the hypothetical ellipsis would explain him in a way he does not accept. Changing his position or silently assuming he is merely reporting someone else's is implausible. The apparent wording also deserves interpretive priority unless a reason for an alternative reconstruction is established.

### Editorial qualifications

The Qurʾānic examples are alleged parallels for possible ellipsis; their exegetical strength is not established here.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-032"></a>
## Introduction note 32 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-032` · **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb p.16 (quoted) · **Original printed source:** [p.174](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/174?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

وسيُشير المصنف إلى تثليث أجزاء القضية

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The promised indication occurs where the author says that if a categorical judgment attributes something to something else or denies it, the proposition is affirmative or negative; the judged-about is its subject (*mawḍūʿ*), what is attributed its predicate (*maḥmūl*), and what signifies the relation its copula (*rābiṭa*). He says nothing about “occurrence or nonoccurrence of the relation” as an additional fourth constituent, either expressly or by implication.

### Editorial qualifications

Quoted future-text concordance. Actual Taftāzānī matn should be checked as a separate T-family source.
