<a id="hwn-intro-033"></a>
## Introduction note 33 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-033` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.174](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/174?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

أي تقييدية إضافية

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

That is, a restrictive relation may be genitive or relational, like the preceding example; or adjectival, as in “a standing man”; or another incomplete construction, such as “the one whose father struck [someone].” Such relations are not identical with an independent complete report.

### Editorial qualifications

Clarifies technical *nisba taqyīdiyya* as an incomplete relation.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-034"></a>
## Introduction note 34 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-034` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.174](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/174?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

اعلم أن من تصور النسبة الحكمية

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

A person who conceives a predicative relation can have several distinct states. First, the conceived image may influence the soul, stirring attraction or repulsion, even when the intellect recognises that the opposite is true. Rhetorical phrases such as “wine is flowing ruby, delightful,” or “honey is bitter and nauseating,” produce this *takhyīl* (imaginative impression).

If it does not have that effect, and the two sides—being and nonbeing—appear evenly balanced with no preference, the state is *shakk* (doubt). If one side is preferred without certitude, the less likely side considered is *wahm* (conjecture) and the more likely side *ẓann* (opinion). Where there is definite judgment, the author distinguishes cases in which the negative side is fixed and those in which the positive side is asserted. He further distinguishes judgments corresponding to reality from those which do not; an entrenched false judgment is *jahl murakkab* (compound ignorance), whereas a judgment agreeing with reality is *yaqīn* (certainty) when immune to further doubt or *taqlīd* (unexamined acceptance) when susceptible to it.

The resulting classification is said to have eight forms. It is not acceptable to label every one as assent in the relevant technical sense. Imaginative impression, doubt, and mere weaker apprehension lack judgment; the glossator also sets apart the negative case as he has defined it. The remaining forms are treated as assent. Therefore “assent” in the original division must be taken broadly enough to embrace probable opinion (*ẓann*) rather than only the highest certitude (*yaqīn*). The proposed eightfold count is a local classification of mental states; its labels are not automatically equivalent to those of all other logic schools.

### Editorial qualifications

Source eightfold taxonomy; exact distribution of 'falsehood' vs 'compound ignorance' merits further clarification but sequence/conditions are rendered.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-035"></a>
## Introduction note 35 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-035` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.175](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/175?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله بمعنى القسمة

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The clarification “in the sense of division” prevents a misunderstanding of the author's sentence. Because the form *iftiʿāl* is often intransitive, one might take *iqtisām* to mean “accepting division,” then insert an omitted preposition before “necessity and acquisition.” That would yield: conception and assent are divided by necessity and acquisition.

But, the glossator argues, al-Zamakhsharī's *Asās al-Balāgha* also uses *iqtisām* transitively in the sense of actively dividing. There is then no reason to supply the omitted preposition. Conception and assent *divide* necessity and acquisition. This does not mean conception is assigned wholly to one and assent wholly to the other—as though one person were allotting two different sorts of property to separate recipients. Rather, each of conception and assent receives a share in each of the two kinds. It resembles the way a noun is divided into definite and indefinite, declinable and indeclinable, explicit and pronominal, simple and construct forms.

On the intended reading, then, both conception and assent can be immediate and both can be acquired. The syntactic construction conveys the intended logical division, but not by taking the whole of necessity as conception and the whole of acquisition as assent, or conversely.

### Editorial qualifications

Transitivity of *iqtisām* used to preserve both taṣawwur and taṣdīq under both necessary and acquired cognition.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-036"></a>
## Introduction note 36 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-036` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh Muḥammad al-Karmī (modern quoted insertion) · **Original printed source:** [p.175](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/175?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

ونجد كثيرا من أفعال باب الافتعال متعدية بأنفسها

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

Many verbs belonging to the pattern *iftiʿāl* govern their objects directly: for example, verbs meaning “carry it,” “take it back,” “cut it out,” or “pluck it.” Their direct transitivity need not be explained artificially by positing that they incorporate the meanings of other transitive verbs. The author regards that manoeuvre as an arbitrary complication.

### Editorial qualifications

Explicitly MODERN printed attribution to al-Karmī. Count as a printed numbered item but never as sixteenth-century al-Yazdī or early glossator speech.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-037"></a>
## Introduction note 37 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-037` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.175](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/175?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

هو ما يكتسب بلا نظر والكسبى ما يكتسب بالنظر

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The immediate or necessary is what is obtained without discursive inquiry (*naẓar*); the acquired or theoretical is what is obtained through inquiry. This formulation differs from the familiar definitions: necessary knowledge is what does not *depend* on inquiry, and theoretical knowledge what does depend on it. The distinction matters for knowledge that is ordinarily acquired discursively but might also be attained in a sudden intuition (*ḥads*) or by a specially endowed intellect. On the dependence formulation, that particular occurrence would cease to be theoretical and the definitions would fail either to include all the intended cases or to exclude the others. Describing how knowledge actually occurs—without or through inquiry—avoids the immediate ambiguity.

### Editorial qualifications

A conceptual objection about *dependency* vs *mode of acquisition*, not an independent proof of a metaphysical 'sacred soul'.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-038"></a>
## Introduction note 38 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-038` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.175](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/175?lang=ar) · [p.176](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/176?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

يعني أن معنى كلام المصنف ظاهر

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The surface wording could seem to mean that conception and assent divide necessity and acquisition, making the first pair the *dividers* and the second pair what is *divided*. The principal purpose, however, is to show that each of conception and assent is divisible into immediate and acquired kinds. This can be understood implicitly in the stated division. If both conception and assent each take shares from the two classes, each must have an immediate kind and an acquired kind, just as a noun divides into definite/indefinite or other opposing grammatical classifications.

The main assertion is thus conveyed by implication (*iltizām*) and by indirect expression (*kināya*), rather than explicitly saying that conception and assent are each of two kinds. The commentator calls an indirect expression better and more eloquent than a bare statement because the conclusion is approached through something entailing it: in favourable cases, the existence of a necessary concomitant provides evidence for the intended meaning. One must nevertheless preserve the difference between an implication that actually follows and a rhetorical interpretation merely alleged to follow.

### Editorial qualifications

The note crosses Q175–176; this is its first-pass full argumentative summary, not independently scanned.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-039"></a>
## Introduction note 39 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-039` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.176](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/176?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله ضمنا وكناية

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

Two explanations of *kināya* were already contrasted in the book's preface: al-Sakkākī and his followers define it as mentioning what necessarily accompanies something while intending the thing accompanied, with the literal meaning still potentially intended; another group reverses that relation. The present construction can support either explanation, because the two relevant divisions entail one another when the terms are understood correctly.

The glossator criticizes an interpretation which reduces the matter to simply reversing the order of a sentence, as in the proverbial “I presented the she-camel to the pool” instead of “I presented the pool to the she-camel.” Reversal is not in principle incompatible with indirect expression; one can use a reversal *and* speak by kināya. The critic might have intended indirect expression effected by a reversal, but that is not the same as defining kināya as reversal itself.

### Editorial qualifications

A distinction between structural inversion and indirect predication; explicitly preserves possibility of both.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-040"></a>
## Introduction note 40 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-040` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.176](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/176?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله وهي أبلغ وأحسن من التصريح

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

Why is an indirect expression said to be more eloquent and beautiful than explicit speech? One answer likens it to establishing a claim by evidence. From the existence of the thing accompanied (*malzūm*) one may infer its necessary concomitant (*lāzim*), because the one cannot exist without the other. That explanation works readily for the account of kināya in which the thing accompanied is mentioned and the concomitant intended.

For al-Sakkākī's direction of inference—from the concomitant to the thing it accompanies—the explanation is less straightforward. A concomitant may be more general than the thing for which it is a concomitant, and the existence of what is general does not prove a specific instance. The argument can be repaired if the concomitant and its referent are coextensive or otherwise mutually entailing. Al-Sakkākī is cited as recognising the dependence of the inference on that equality. The full theory of rhetorical evidence would require more discussion than this note permits.

### Editorial qualifications

A rigorous scope qualification: a necessary consequence need not be convertible unless coextensive.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-041"></a>
## Introduction note 41 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-041` · **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb pp.16–17 (lexical gloss) · **Original printed source:** [p.176](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/176?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

جشم يجشم ... جشامة الأمر

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The verb *jashima* means to undertake something with exertion or hardship. In the causative form *jashshamahu* or *ajshamahu*, it means to impose that burdensome task upon another person. This explains the earlier phrase about the effort required to construct a proof.

### Editorial qualifications

Short lexical item; no historical speaker identity beyond quoted al-Taqrīb heading.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-042"></a>
## Introduction note 42 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-042` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.176](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/176?lang=ar) · [p.177](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/177?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله كما ارتكبه القوم

</div>

### Working English (partial)

The usual proof that some conceptions and assents are immediate and others theoretical argues by excluding two extremes. They cannot **all** be immediate, because in fact some objects of conception and some matters of judgment require deliberate inquiry. Nor can they **all** be theoretical, because if every cognition required a prior cognition, the acquisition of knowledge would be trapped in circular dependence or endless regress.

If a cognition A requires B, B requires C and C in turn requires A, the supposed acquisition of A would require A itself to precede its own acquisition—an impossibility. The infinite regress also seems to demand a completion of infinitely many prerequisites before the present cognition can occur. The glossator develops the distinctions between circularity and regress and the assumptions behind the impossibility of each.

An objection raises a possible eternal history of intellect: an infinite series of preparatory cognitions might be spread over an infinitely long past, with each preparing the next rather than being jointly present at one time. A response says this is unavailable on the theological premises which reject the eternity of souls and the world; the argument is therefore conditional upon those disputed premises and does not by itself prove that every kind of infinite causal sequence is impossible.

A final logical caution is important. The two types of cognition may not be absolutely isolated. If a conception could be acquired by an earlier assent or an assent by an earlier conception, the claim that **every conception** is theoretical might terminate in a necessary assent, and the claim that **every assent** is theoretical might terminate in a necessary conception. The standard proof therefore requires an additional premise restricting the cross-type acquisition routes. Without it, the proposed dilemma does not independently establish the twofold division *within each type*.

### Editorial qualifications

Extensive source Q176→177 with multiple objections and philosophical premises. Significant English analytic translation, but not fully clause-paired; remains PARTIAL.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-043"></a>
## Introduction note 43 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-043` · **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb p.17 (quoted) · **Original printed source:** [p.177](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/177?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله وذلك أي ودليل بداهتها

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The point is to establish that immediate cognitions really occur. Reality is often disclosed to us spontaneously through ordinary dealings with the world. We encounter heat without seeking to know it, conceive what heat is, and learn of fire; we then judge that fire is hot, without having first constructed a deliberate proof. Thus a conception and a judgment can arise without paid effort or systematic inquiry. Thousands of similar everyday examples are available.

Theoretical cognition is equally evident: the very need for canons of inference and methods of evaluating proofs is to acquire and establish conclusions that do not simply arrive of themselves.

### Editorial qualifications

An appeal to spontaneous experience, not an independently established formal classification of particular sensings.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-044"></a>
## Introduction note 44 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-044` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.177](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/177?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله كتصور الحرارة والبرودة

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

By the conception of heat and cold is meant the intellect's grasp of the *universal concept*, acquired by means of particular sensations of heat and cold, not those particular sensations themselves. Heat itself can be present in the bodily organ which bears the power of touch. Its being present in that physical way is not, on this commentator's theory, knowledge, since knowledge is the form received in the intellect from the object.

There is no inconsistency between calling these concepts immediate and later giving the properties or characteristics of heat and cold in a scientific discussion. The latter treatment explains their attributes and judgments concerning them, rather than supplying for the first time the elementary apprehension needed to think of them.

### Editorial qualifications

Historical distinction between the causal occasion in sensation and the universal concept abstracted by intellect.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-045"></a>
## Introduction note 45 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-045` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.177](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/177?lang=ar) · [p.178](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/178?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

الملك جسم نوراني علوي

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The note describes an angel, in the inherited cosmological account, as a luminous upper-world body capable of taking different forms except those of a dog and pig; a jinn is described as a fiery lower-world body capable even of those forms. The spirit, by contrast, is described as an immaterial substance connected to the body as rose-water is connected to a rose.

These are quoted definitions within a premodern theological/cosmological vocabulary, not empirical anatomical propositions or universally agreed metaphysical definitions.

### Editorial qualifications

Short source crosses Q177–178; its cosmological assertions are faithfully attributed without modern factual endorsement.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-046"></a>
## Introduction note 46 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-046` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.178](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/178?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

اعلم أن ضرورية التصديق ونظريته عند الحكماء

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

For the philosophers, assent is necessary or theoretical with respect to the act of judgment alone; they do not include the prior conceptions of the two terms and their relation in this classification. For al-Rāzī, by contrast, assent is a compound of four elements. Its being necessary therefore requires all four to be necessary; it is theoretical if at least one of its components is theoretical, even when the others are not. The same consequence holds for the followers of the view associated with the author of *al-Kashf*.

The extension of “necessary assent” is accordingly broader on the philosophers' simple-act account than on the composite accounts, while “theoretical assent” is broader on the composite accounts. A change in what counts as the *thing classified* changes the extension of its necessary and theoretical species.

### Editorial qualifications

Formal inclusion claim derives from different definitions of assent; source does not count all species as identical.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-047"></a>
## Introduction note 47 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-047` · **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb p.17 (quoted) · **Original printed source:** [p.178](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/178?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

هو ملاحظة المعقول لتحصيل المجهول

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The definition of inquiry (*naẓar*) as “considering an intelligible to obtain an unknown” would, the critic argues, be clearer if it said “considering something known to obtain something unknown.” The latter immediately conveys the thought that an item already disclosed is used to discover something concealed. The phrase “the intelligible” instead suggests what has already been apprehended by the rational faculty, without making the inferential transition as transparent.

The principal commentator gives several reasons for choosing “intelligible.” First, “knowledge” (*ʿilm*) is used in multiple senses, such as a form in the intellect, assent and certainty, and equivocal language in a definition is undesirable. The critic regards the list as verbal proliferation: knowledge properly refers to the disclosure of reality. Second, reasoning proceeds in intelligibles, especially universals, not bare particulars; an isolated particular is too determined to serve as a universal mediating term by which another is inferred. Third, the term maintains balanced rhyme with “the unknown.”

The critic suspects that the third reason was the chief motive. He adds that a particular is neither a suitable general means of acquiring a distinct particular nor usually something acquired by using another particular as a middle, because the individuating conditions separate them. The argument concerns the logical role of middle terms, not the claim that particular events can never be known by signs in ordinary life.

### Editorial qualifications

Rendered substance of the quoted al-Taqrīb note; traditional inference distinction is source-attributed.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-048"></a>
## Introduction note 48 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-048` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.178](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/178?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

أي بطريق القصد كما هو المتبادر

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

“Through inquiry” should mean through an act of deliberate intention, as the wording and final purpose suggest. The definition is therefore not refuted by a case of sudden intuition (*ḥads*), in which the arranged premises arise all at once; in such an event the apprehension is not brought about by a deliberately chosen discursive procedure, but occurs without that voluntary direction.

### Editorial qualifications

Intention distinguishes formal inquiry from sudden intuition; no independent psychometric claim.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-049"></a>
## Introduction note 49 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-049` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.178](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/178?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله نحو الأمر المعقول

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The intelligible under consideration may be a conception or an assent; and the assent may be certain, probable or even mistaken in the specified sense. The expression is broad enough to include one known item or several, since a definition may employ a single term or a composite explanation. The definite article is therefore generic, indicating the class rather than a single preselected item.

### Editorial qualifications

Short scope clarification; note50 starts on Q178 but continues beyond, left for next stage.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-050"></a>
## Introduction note 50 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-050` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.178](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/178?lang=ar) · [p.179](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/179?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله لتحصيل أمر غير معلوم

</div>

### Working English (partial)

The unknown sought through reflection may be either conceptual or assertoric. To discover the meaning of “human,” one may arrange the previously understood concepts “animal” and “rational”; to establish that the world is originated, one may assemble “the world changes” and “whatever changes is originated.” The starting elements must already be known in the relevant respect. Yet the result cannot already be known in precisely the respect in which it is sought, since that would be obtaining what one already possesses. It must be identified under another description so that inquiry can be directed toward it.

The note recalls al-Rāzī’s dilemma: if the object is known, inquiry is redundant; if utterly unknown, one cannot seek it. Knowing it in one respect and not in another appears to answer this dilemma, but the glossator reports a further objection: the known aspect cannot be sought as unknown, while the unknown aspect is not yet available to intention. A fuller dispute involving Sharaf al-Dīn al-Marāghī is expressly set aside by the source.

### Editorial qualifications

The closing Arabic explicitly declines to treat al-Marāghī’s objection in detail. This is a substantial partial translation, not an invented completion of that debate.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-051"></a>
## Introduction note 51 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-051` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.179](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/179?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله وفي العدول عن لفظ المعلوم

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The statement about departing from the word “known” supplies an answer to the objection that the author did not define inquiry as considering the known to obtain the unknown, even though knowledge and ignorance are the natural opposites.

### Editorial qualifications

Short printed gloss; the proposed alternative is attributed to the objector.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-052"></a>
## Introduction note 52 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-052` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.179](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/179?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله منها التحرز عن استعمال اللفظ المشترك

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

“Knowledge” has several technical uses. It may signify a form present in intellect, encompassing conception and assent; it may also mean firm, unshakable, reality-conforming conviction, or belief in a broad sense including opinion, compound ignorance and imitation. A definition using such a shared expression without an indicator risks being read in the wrong sense. That is the reason advanced for choosing “intelligible” instead of “known.”

### Editorial qualifications

The list reports distinct inherited usages; it does not make compound ignorance genuine knowledge in every technical vocabulary.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-053"></a>
## Introduction note 53 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-053` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.179](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/179?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

أما الأول فلأن الجزئي إما أن يكون محسوسا

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

A particular can be apprehended through the five outward senses or through the inward powers traditionally enumerated as common sense, imagination, estimation, the compositive power and memory. Simply arranging several particular sensations does not produce another particular sensation or a universal as a logical inference. Nor does joining universals as such yield a determinate individual. On this account particulars are neither logical premises acquiring another particular nor conclusions acquired as particulars. “Intelligible” therefore points toward universals, whereas “known” can cover both particular and universal objects.

### Editorial qualifications

A historical restriction on *formal inferential acquisition*, not a denial of ordinary knowledge of particulars through signs.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-054"></a>
## Introduction note 54 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-054` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.180](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/180?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

في بعض النسخ كتب هذه الحاشية قبل حاشية قوله وقد يقع فيه الخطاء

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

Some copies place this marginal note before the comment on “error may occur in it.” If that order was not a copyist’s inadvertence, it may have been chosen to bring the remarks on the subject of logic directly beside the gloss beginning “and its subject ...,” preserving the flow of the explanation. Another suggested motive is the beginner’s desire to learn the general rule as soon as possible. These are hypotheses about order, not independently collated readings of identified manuscripts.

### Editorial qualifications

Reported copy-order difference only; neither source copy nor reading has been authenticated.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-055"></a>
## Introduction note 55 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-055` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.180](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/180?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

وقيل رومي موضوع في الأصل

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

One account says the word *qānūn* was originally a Greek or Roman expression for the ruling instrument used to draw writing lines, or more generally a writing ruler or grid. The *Qāmūs* defines it as the measure of anything; its plural is *qawānīn*.

### Editorial qualifications

Lexical attribution is reported, not independent etymological confirmation.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-056"></a>
## Introduction note 56 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-056` · **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb, printed pp.17–18 (quoted) · **Original printed source:** [p.180](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/180?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله لمسطر الكتابة

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

A writing ruler straightens lines on a page. In technical usage a *qānūn* is a universal proposition from which the rules for many particular instances of its subject can be learned. For example, the grammatical rule “Every agent is nominative” applies to the agent in “Zayd stood,” “ʿAmr sat,” and indefinitely many other sentences.

### Editorial qualifications

Short quoted al-Taqrīb note; grammatical rule and its applications are examples, not a claim that every rule of logic concerns Arabic grammar.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-057"></a>
## Introduction note 57 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-057` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.180](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/180?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله وفي الاصطلاح قضية كلية

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The literal writing ruler and the technical universal rule are appropriately related: each is one thing by means of which many distinct results are attained. The technical use may be a settled conventional signification or an explicit metaphor; the former is preferred here.

### Editorial qualifications

Competing theories of technical lexical usage remain distinct.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-058"></a>
## Introduction note 58 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-058` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.180](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/180?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله تعرف منها أحكام جزئيات موضوعها

</div>

### Working English (partial)

Apply the universal proposition as the major premise of an easily available minor premise. In “Zayd struck,” identify Zayd as the agent; combine “Zayd is an agent” with “Every agent is nominative,” and infer “Zayd is nominative.” The rule makes particular applications potentially available in advance; supplying the minor premise brings each to explicit actuality.

But the applications need not all be theoretical or unknown. Some are immediate, such as the familiar productivity of the first syllogistic figure. A rule still has the *capacity* to yield them, even if an individual conclusion is already self-evident. The description “judgments are learned from it” should accordingly be read in terms of suitability to support such judgments, not as asserting that every application actually requires learning.

### Editorial qualifications

Long source has reported variant «يتعرف بها»; it is not an independently certified manuscript reading. First-pass argument rendered, but source-line collation remains incomplete.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-059"></a>
## Introduction note 59 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-059` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.180](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/180?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

أي قضية كلية إطلاقا لاسم الجزء

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

This means a universal proposition. The term for the decisive part is applied to the whole, just as “neck” can stand for a person or “eye” for a watcher. The part in question is central to the whole’s existence and use.

### Editorial qualifications

Brief metonymic explanation, not a new universal law.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-060"></a>
## Introduction note 60 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-060` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.180](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/180?lang=ar) · [p.181](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/181?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله وقد ينتهي إلى نقيضها

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

“It may end in its contradictory” is taken broadly: the outcome may be opposed to the earlier result by affirmation and negation, privation and possession, real contrariety, correlatives or a related form of incompatibility. The author illustrates error in the thoughts that generate assents because it is more conspicuous there. Error can also occur in the formation of conceptions, as disagreements about essential and descriptive definitions show. The omitted parallel example can therefore be understood by analogy.

### Editorial qualifications

Note begins p180, closes on p181 before (61); distinguish strict contradiction from the broader listed oppositions.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-061"></a>
## Introduction note 61 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-061` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.181](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/181?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

القدم كعنب ضد الحدوث

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

*Qidam* is opposed to origination. The word *ʿālam* is treated in one account as a collective with no singular from its own form. It can signify a group of rational creatures, the totality of created things, the classes of angels and the two responsible kinds, or what reveals the Creator through substances and accidents. Different proposed etymologies derive it from a sign (*ʿalāma*) or knowledge (*ʿilm*).

### Editorial qualifications

Several lexicographical interpretations are juxtaposed; no single proposed etymology is certified.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-062"></a>
## Introduction note 62 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-062` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.181](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/181?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

ولا يخفى أن هذا أعم من أن يكون أحد الفكرين ناشئا من شخص

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

Contradictory results of reasoning can belong to different persons, since thinkers disagree; but they can also arise within one person's intellectual history, as the same inquirer changes judgments at different times. Even established scholars can adopt new positions. The case is not confined to public disputes between two writers.

### Editorial qualifications

Distinguishes disagreement across persons from a change in the same person over time.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-063"></a>
## Introduction note 63 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-063` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.181](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/181?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله وإلا لزم اجتماع النقيضين

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

When a person directly recalls having reasoned to one conclusion and then to its opposite, the conflict of the judgments is accessible by introspection. In disputes between different people, words alone do not guarantee that their internal beliefs contradict: a speaker may utter a position for display or some other reason. Error also affects conception, as shown by disagreement about *ḥadd* and *rasm*. The author's emphasis on assent may rest on its greater obviousness or on leaving the parallel conceptual case to analogy.

### Editorial qualifications

A distinction between contradictory utterances and actual opposed assents; it does not make all self-reports infallible.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-064"></a>
## Introduction note 64 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-064` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.181](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/181?lang=ar) · [p.182](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/182?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله فلا بد من قاعدة كلية

</div>

### Working English (partial)

What is sought in practice is a judgment about a particular act of reasoning: is this inference sound or defective? Since no finite teacher can enumerate all future cases, the discipline supplies universal rules which can be applied when a case arises. A possible objection is that someone might gain insight without discursive reasoning, through purification of attention or intellectual disclosure. The reply restricts the claim to the *discursive reasoner qua reasoner*: logic is needed for monitoring such reasoning, not asserted to be the only possible route to every form of knowledge.

Another objection says that one might already know by immediate rational awareness which inferential methods are correct, and errors arise merely through inattentiveness. The reply invokes the occurrence of errors by sincere inquirers who aim at truth. In the context of using acquired inferential methods, unaided common sense does not always suffice to distinguish the sound from the defective.

### Editorial qualifications

Two-page note with further wording requiring a literal Arabic clause audit; source's restricted necessity of logic is preserved.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-065"></a>
## Introduction note 65 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-065` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.182](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/182?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

الناس في الأصل أناس حذفت همزته تخفيفا

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The word *al-nās* has been explained as originally *unās*, with its hamza suppressed for ease, especially with the definite article. Another account calls it a collective lacking a singular of the same form and associates it with movement (*naws*). A cited lexicon allows a broader meaning covering jinn and humans, but in this passage the human community is intended.

### Editorial qualifications

Reports alternative lexical derivations; no single one independently established.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-066"></a>
## Introduction note 66 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-066` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.182](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/182?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله بثلاث مقدمات

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

Why begin the proof that logic is needed by dividing cognition into conception and assent? Would it not suffice to distinguish knowledge acquired by reflection from that which arises without reflection? The answer is that the author wants to establish the need for logic in both its parts: the way leading to unknown conceptions and the way leading to unknown judgments. A genus can have two kinds without each species itself exhibiting both kinds. Without the more detailed initial divisions, one could suppose all conception immediate while only assent required acquisition, or conversely. The desired twofold need would not follow.

### Editorial qualifications

The note later queries whether the final premise proves need for logic itself or need to learn it; its logic is a distinction of conclusions.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-067"></a>
## Introduction note 67 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-067` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.182](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/182?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله فهذه المقدمات الثلاث

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The argument has three premises in its outward presentation but arguably four in substance, because the second combines two propositions: conception and assent each divide into immediate and acquired kinds, and theoretical knowledge is obtained from immediate knowledge.

### Editorial qualifications

A structural unpacking of the argument, not a new manuscript reading.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-068"></a>
## Introduction note 68 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-068` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.182](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/182?lang=ar) · [p.183](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/183?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله فهذه المقدمات الثلاث تفيد احتياج الناس

</div>

### Working English (partial)

A familiar counterargument says that logic itself must be acquired, since otherwise its rules would be immediately evident and there would be no need to study it. But if every acquisition needs a previously acquired logical rule, the acquisition of logic would require another logic, producing a circle or regress. The proposed answer distinguishes the parts: some elementary logical rules are immediate, illustrated by the first syllogistic figure, while others are demonstrable from those basics, as the other figures are derived from the first. Logic is not entirely immediate and not entirely theoretical.

A different formulation alleges that logic *must* be immediately known to avoid the regress. Even if granted, that would show only that learning it is unnecessary, not that reasoning can dispense with it. A knower may rely on an already possessed logical principle when reaching further conclusions. The source warns that this dispute is difficult and sensitive to the distinction between possessing a norm, acquiring it and using it.

### Editorial qualifications

Long note p182→183 with two forms of objection, dialectical replies and internal nuance. This is an English analytical rendering, NOT full clause-by-clause source translation.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-069"></a>
## Introduction note 69 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-069` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.183](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/183?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله وعلم من هذا تعريف المنطق

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The need for logic reveals its objective: to protect the inquirer in the activity for which the discipline is needed. Explaining that objective gives a descriptive account (*rasm*) of the science through its end. An objection says that a proper identifying characteristic must be coextensive with what it describes, whereas the same end might belong to many disciplines. The reply specifies the intended coextensive end rather than a purpose shared indiscriminately.

### Editorial qualifications

Purpose/definition argument should not be confused with an essential *ḥadd*.

<a id="hwn-intro-070"></a>
## Introduction note 70 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-070` · **Reported voice:** al-Shamsiyya (quoted marginal dictum) · **Original printed source:** [p.183](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/183?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

وإنما قال تعصم مراعاتها ولم يقل نفسها

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The author rightly says that *observing* logical rules protects thought, not that the mere possession of logic does. Trained logicians sometimes err because they fail to use the instrument.

### Editorial qualifications

Quoted short source under Shamsiyya; does not ascribe psychological infallibility to practitioners.

<a id="hwn-intro-071"></a>
## Introduction note 71 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-071` · **Reported voice:** Mixed printed note and quoted al-Taqrīb, pp.18–19 · **Original printed source:** [p.183](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/183?lang=ar) · [p.184](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/184?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله فهاهنا ... وموضوعه المعلوم التصوري والتصديقي

</div>

### Working English (partial)

The original introduction promised three matters: the need for logic, its definition, and its subject. The first two have been addressed; the third identifies known conceptions and known assents *insofar as they can lead to unknown conceptions and assents*. This qualification excludes merely considering a known item without its ability to advance inquiry.

A later quoted critic accepts that a particular conceived object, qua particular, may not serve as an inferential middle. But he challenges the claim that some known propositions, such as “Fire is hot,” are incapable of leading to further conclusions. Combined with a general rule about the effects of heat, that statement can yield a result about fire. Its being obvious is irrelevant to whether it can function as a premise. The distinction should be between *capacity to lead* and actual profundity, not between respectable theoretical propositions and ordinary judgments.

### Editorial qualifications

The Q183→184 passage has a separate extended al-Taqrīb criticism; abbreviated, not complete translation. Preserve separate source voice.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-072"></a>
## Introduction note 72 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-072` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.184](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/184?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله موضوع العلم

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The commentator first defines the subject of a science generally, then applies the definition to logic. This provides a universal principle for the particular case: the items whose intrinsic attributes are investigated in logic are the known conceptual and assertoric objects; whatever has its intrinsic attributes investigated in a science is that science's subject; therefore these objects constitute logic's subject.

### Editorial qualifications

Explicit reconstructed inference is the glossator's application of the general subject formula.

<a id="hwn-intro-073"></a>
## Introduction note 73 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-073` · **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb, p.19, with separately introduced Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī discussion · **Original printed source:** [p.184](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/184?lang=ar) · [p.185](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/185?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله موضوع العلم ما يبحث فيه عن العوارض الذاتية

</div>

### Working English (partial)

An intrinsic accident belongs to a subject through its own nature, or through something not foreign to that subject in the relevant explanatory sense; a foreign accident depends on an extraneous intermediary. Examples include wonder in human beings, occupying space through being a body, speech through rationality, and laughter associated with wonder. The source develops a list differentiated by whether the mediating factor is a constituent, equal in extension, more general, more specific or wholly separate. Cases mediated by something narrower or external are more readily classified as foreign; those mediated through a constituent or coextensive feature are disputed.

A separately introduced longer marginal intervention then compares older and later classifications. It reports agreement on three intrinsic and three foreign cases, with the case mediated by a more general constituent disputed; it also argues that the science should investigate its subject's intrinsic accidents because these genuinely describe that subject rather than another subject to which the property belongs more immediately.

### Editorial qualifications

Long Q184→185 technical register, NOT full line-by-line bilingual collation; modern/earlier speakers explicitly distinct. Follow-up source should preserve seven- or eightfold enumerations exactly.

### Adjacent UNNUMBERED quoted gloss — separate source voice, not additional numbered note

**Reported attribution:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī, independently introduced extended gloss after no.73. Its Arabic here is a short locator/excerpt, NOT a full Arabic transcription; the English is a selected explanation and is NOT an authenticated historical quotation.

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

اعلم : ان العوارض قسمان : اعراض ذاتية واعراض عرضية

</div>

A separate intervention distinguishes intrinsic and extrinsic attributes by the type and extension of the intermediary: the essence, an equal or more general constituent, or an external equal, broader, narrower or disjoint intermediary. The traditional classificatory dispute specifically concerns attributes through a more general internal constituent. It argues that each science studies the genuine properties of its own subject. This is an editorial synopsis of an unnumbered continuation, not a complete Arabic translation.

<a id="hwn-intro-074"></a>
## Introduction note 74 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-074` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.185](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/185?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

الضمير المجرور في قوله فيه يعود إلى العلم

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

The pronoun in “in it” refers to the science, whereas “its accidents” refers to the entity identified as its subject. The subject of medicine is the human body in respect of health and disease; that of jurisprudence is the acts of responsible persons in respect of legal permissibility, prohibition, validity and invalidity; that of grammar is the word and utterance in respect of inflection and construction.

### Editorial qualifications

Examples explain the grammatical referents of the definition; not every discipline is thereby reduced to one undifferentiated subject.

<a id="hwn-intro-075"></a>
## Introduction note 75 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-075` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.185](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/185?lang=ar) · [p.186](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/186?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

أي يرجع فيه إليها وذلك إما بجعل موضوع العلم موضوع المسألة

</div>

### Working English (partial)

A particular question in a science may take the science's general subject as its own subject, or one of its kinds. Thus natural philosophy may investigate bodies as such or a kind of body; the scientific question may also establish an attribute connected through a broader consideration provided that the inquiry does not exceed the proper scope of the science. Comparable examples are drawn from jurisprudential judgments about intoxicants and natural philosophy's account of intermediate rest between two rectilinear motions. The argument answers a worry that some properties considered by sciences depend on narrower conditions and therefore would not qualify as intrinsic accidents of the general subject.

### Editorial qualifications

Long note crosses Q185→186, source distinction between subject and species of subject needs full clause-level check; keep partial.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-076"></a>
## Introduction note 76 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-076` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.186](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/186?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

أراد بالأمر ما يعم الداخل والخارج

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

“Intermediary” includes both internal and external factors. When it is required to be equal in extension to the subject, only two relevant cases remain: an equal internal constituent and an equal external property. Explaining the equal intermediary as necessarily external would therefore be too narrow.

### Editorial qualifications

Six-category partition reduced by equality condition; source examples include speech arising through rationality.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-077"></a>
## Introduction note 77 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-077` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.186](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/186?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله كالضحك الذي يعرض

</div>

### Working English (partial)

The example of laughter arising through astonishment is intended in terms of a capacity or potentiality. Otherwise, if astonishment means an actual episode, it is narrower than humanity since humans are not always astonished; if it means only a capacity, an actual act of laughing may require more than that capacity. Taking both laughter and astonishment in the relevant potential sense is proposed as the repair.

A further question asks whether “one who is astonished” names an intelligible notion distinct from the individual, or the individual himself. On the first reading one risks assigning laughter first to the individual rather than to the abstract notion; on the second the intermediary becomes identical with the bearer rather than a genuinely explanatory middle. The source records this difficulty without decisively resolving it.

### Editorial qualifications

Substantive translation of both principal objections; note ends with an expressed invitation to reconsider.

أي عن عوارضه وهكذا الحال في قوله بل يبحث عن المعرف والحجة

<a id="hwn-intro-080"></a>
## Introduction note 80 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-080` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.186](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/186?lang=ar) · [p.187](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/187?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

لا يقال إن البحث عن المعرف والحجة من هذه الحيثية

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

An objection says that investigating definition and proof *insofar as they lead to the unknown* is just investigating the leading itself; but if being a vehicle of discovery already forms part of logic's subject, the subject and its constituents cannot be investigated as though they were properties of themselves. The reply distinguishes **the fact or capacity of leading to an unknown**—the qualification defining the subject—from **the manner in which the leading occurs**, which is an attribute open to investigation. This avoids an identical subject/property claim.

### Editorial qualifications

Two distinct respects—leading as subject condition and manner of leading as investigated attribute.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-081"></a>
## Introduction note 81 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-081` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.187](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/187?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

فيكون من باب تسمية الدال باسم المدلول

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

Expressions such as “rational animal” are called definitions, and symbolic premise sequences are called syllogisms, but strictly it is their *meanings* that explain a nature or establish an inference. The words communicate those meanings and may receive their names by association. Logic's primary subject is therefore the intelligible contents of definitions and arguments, not sounds or written signs considered independently.

### Editorial qualifications

Do not infer that attention to logical language is irrelevant; it is instrumental to understanding the content.

---

<a id="hwn-intro-082"></a>
## Introduction note 82 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-INTRO-082` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.187](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/187?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

قوله من قبيل تسمية السبب

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

A proof can be called a *ḥujja* because it causes victory in argument. This names the cause by the name of its result, as when rain is called “vegetation” because it brings vegetation about. The analogy explains the conventional transfer, not the literal identity of proof and victory.

### Editorial qualifications

The section ends on printed p187; p188 begins a different titled local-note series on signification.

# Tahdhīb al-Manṭiq — Glosses on Conception — Signification, local notes 1–20

**GNAMMA Stage111 · 9 November 2026 · source-linked Arabic locator and working English corpus; NOT a photographically collated Arabic critical edition.**

**Section:** H-DALALA: حواشي التصورات « بحث الدلالات » · **Qum printed:** 188–191. The historical notes have their own LOCAL numbering, with stable IDs HWN-DALALA-001–HWN-DALALA-020. They are separate from any equally numbered notes in other sections.

**Actual coverage:** 20 historical numbered notes, **14 complete working first-pass English**, 6 explicitly incomplete (numbers 1, 12, 13, 14, 15, 18); 1,116 English words in note fields. **0 full Arabic transcriptions**: every Arabic phrase below is a SHORT locator lemma only. Every note remains scanCertified=false, and **no full printed page** is manuscript-/photograph-certified. Different speakers and quoted modern passages must not be silently assigned to Yazdī's own main commentary.

**What remains:** The complete H later-gloss print continues to Qum p400; English statuses and Arabic source certifications are different objectives. [Cumulative recovery handoff](../HANDOFF_LATEST.md) · [Research progress](../progress.html).

<a id="hwn-dalala-001"></a>
## Signification note 1 — PARTIAL — incomplete source translation

**ID:** `HWN-DALALA-001` · **Reported voice:** al-Taqrīb, printed p.20 (quoted) · **Original printed source:** [p.188](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/188?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

حواشي التصورات بحث الدلالات قوله دلالة اللفظ

</div>

### Working English (partial)

Logic is primarily concerned with known concepts and judgments as means of acquiring further concepts and judgments. That purpose does not itself require studying the sounds of words. Nevertheless, traditional logic textbooks begin by discussing simple and compound expressions, univocal and gradated terms, and related distinctions because they assist the presentation of conceptual inquiry.

Signification in general occurs when cognition of one thing leads to cognition of another. Conventional verbal signification depends on the designation of an expression for a meaning; a sound never conventionally assigned to a meaning does not possess that particular signification. Within verbal conventional signification, *muṭābaqa* indicates the whole meaning assigned, *taḍammun* indicates a constituent part, and *iltizām* indicates an accompanying implication outside the meaning itself.

### Editorial qualifications

Long source note abbreviated, not a full literal English translation. The al-Taqrīb author should not be conflated with Yazdī.

---

<a id="hwn-dalala-002"></a>
## Signification note 2 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-DALALA-002` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.188](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/188?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

وذلك لأن ما يبين ويعرف ماهية الإنسان هو معنى الحيوان الناطق

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

What defines a human as a rational animal is the *meaning* of that expression, not merely the pronunciation. Otherwise someone ignorant of the language's word assignments would grasp the definition simply on hearing the words. Similarly, what demonstrates the world's origination is the understood content of the premises “the world changes” and “whatever changes is originated,” not their sounds.

### Editorial qualifications

Conceptual content versus linguistic vehicle.

**ID:** `HWN-DALALA-005` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.189](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/189?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

<a id="hwn-dalala-006"></a>
## Signification note 6 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-DALALA-006` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.189](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/189?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

الدلالة الالتزامية مهجورة في الحدود التامة

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

Logic needs an account of signification because technical rules concerning definitions distinguish whole, partial and implicative meanings. In a complete essential definition, an external implication cannot replace the defining content; the use of constituent meanings likewise has restrictions. The five predicables belong to the study of simple meanings, requiring a prior distinction between simple and compound expressions. Figurative and equivocal expressions are normally avoided in definitions unless a clarifying indicator is present. Univocal and gradated terms also matter: some allow a gradated expression to function as a genus while others deny it. These controversies explain the conventional introductory study of language.

### Editorial qualifications

First-pass paraphrase of the note's instructional dependency chain, not certification of every reported technical opinion.

---

<a id="hwn-dalala-007"></a>
## Signification note 7 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-DALALA-007` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.189](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/189?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

لا من حيث إنها موجودة أو أصوات

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

Expressions are not studied here simply because they exist, are sounds, are accidents rather than substances, are necessary or possible, or are temporally enduring or successive. They are considered only in the respect relevant to logical teaching and signification. The note prefers this pedagogical restriction to treating words themselves as an essential component of logic.

### Editorial qualifications

Distinguishes object studied under a qualification from word as physical object.

---

<a id="hwn-dalala-008"></a>
## Signification note 8 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-DALALA-008` · **Reported voice:** Mīrzā Muḥammad ʿAlī · **Original printed source:** [p.189](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/189?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

أراد بالعلم العلم الحصولي المنقسم إلى التصور والتصديق

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

Knowledge here means acquired or representational knowledge divided into conception and assent. Verbal signs may therefore indicate simple or compound meanings, including incomplete possessive and attributive combinations and complete performative or reportable clauses.

### Editorial qualifications

The different kinds of compound expression are listed by rhetorical and logical function.

---

<a id="hwn-dalala-009"></a>
## Signification note 9 — COMPLETE FIRST-PASS English

**ID:** `HWN-DALALA-009` · **Reported voice:** Shaykh ʿAbd al-Raḥīm · **Original printed source:** [p.189](https://ablibrary.net/book_content/b/12170/189?lang=ar) · **Arabic:** short locator ONLY. **Independent witness/photo certification:** NO.

### Short Arabic locator (NOT entire note)

<div lang="ar" dir="rtl">

إن كان منشأ الحيثية المذكورة في تعريف الدلالة وضع الواضع

</div>

### Working English (first pass)

When the signifying relation arises because a convention-maker has assigned a sign to a meaning, the indication is conventional (*waḍʿiyya*). The note also mentions different pronunciations of *ḥasab* depending on whether it follows a preposition, with a rare poetic alternative.

### Editorial qualifications

Conventional causation is not the same thing as a rational proof from an effect.
